To get help on usage of sidelobe program, try one of the following:
No help available for unrecognized option:  '%s'
Program to compute contribution of sidelobes.  Usage:
... Sidelobe program for NRAO GBT and 140-foot telescopes ...
Uncertainties in strayscans from changes in input parameters: Summary:
======================================================================
The largest uncertainty appears to be from our lack of complete knowledge of the
sidelobe pattern for the telescopes.  Errors in computed sidelobe patterns as of
this version may yield errors up to about 30%% of the resulting sidelobe
spectrum (i.e., of the stray radiation spectrum, or "strayscan").
The "sidelobes" are considered to begin about a degree from the center of the
beam.  For pointings in bright, crowded regions (e.g., the galactic plane), the
asymmetry in the GBT beam may cause spectra to vary by of order one or two
percent when obtained on different dates or times (due to rotation of the GBT
beam relative to the sky).  This "near-beam" effect cannot be corrected by this
program (due to the coarser grid of the sky LD-survey).  In regions of strong
but position-dependent signal, it may be comparable to the size of the computed
"strayscan" correction, over quite a wide velocity range around the Local
At present, the GBT dish and secondary mirror are modelled, but any effects of
the arm are ignored in the computation of the GBT beam, as they should be small.
If snow or ice accumulates in the GBT dish, this may affect the beam and its
sidelobes in ways that cannot of course be estimated by this program.
Also, the program assumes that no radiation is picked up from anywhere below
the horizon, which may not always be as good an approximation as one would
wish, particularly in snowy/icy conditions.
Any errors in the LD-survey spectra will of course propagate through, but these
should average out to a large extent.  A very few of the LD-survey spectra have
normalization errors of up to a factor of 1.5, but only a small fraction of the
spectra appear to have normalization errors even at the ten percent level.  A
very few LD-survey spectra have large spurious features (examplees: a 2 K peak
at -230 < v < -90 km/s at l=132.5, b=47.0; a 3.5 K peak at -260 < v < -180 K at
l=138.5, b=37.0).  Tests, where LD-survey spectra were re-calibrated in some
regions where other data were available (and known spurious LD-survey features
were removed), suggest that even when such LD-survey errors are not corrected,
they probably yield errors of a few percent or less in the computed strayscans.
There are two regions a few degrees across where many entire LD-survey spectra
seem to be offset by about 4 km/s (peaks are shifted to more negative velocities
compared to other spectra with similar shapes in and around these regions).
 (1) l = -50.0 ... -45.5, b = 43.0 ... 45.0 (plus l = -49.0 and 49.5, b = 45.5):
spectra at most pixels in this region appear to be shifted by 4 km/s compared
to pixels around its border; there are a few exceptions, and a couple of pixels
 (2) l = -60.0 ... -55.5, b = 46.5 ... 50.0 : spectra at about 1/3 of the pixels
in this region appear to be shifted by about 3 km/s.
 - NOTE that reasonable corrections of known LD-survey spurious features and
calibrations can be controlled by sidelobe input options (see, e.g., '--fix-cal'
Even with perfect knowledge of the telescope sidelobes, there would be further
errors in the strayscan of at least a fraction of a percent rms (up to a few
percent maximum) from the 1 km/s LD-survey velocity binning; this might be
increased somewhat due to the 0.5-degree LD-survey sampling grid, and due to
the fact that there would be only a few LD-survey points in the near-sidelobe
region (within a few degrees of the beam), but these latter have not been
Errors due to approximations that yield (much!) faster sidelobe spectrum
evaluation are comparable to the above (rms 0.2%%, max 3%%).
Choice of the sidelobe-vs.-beam cutoff may not be insignificant; shifting this
from a 1.0 degree radius to 0.9 degrees has an effect of rms 2%%, max 14%%,
according to a set of random test cases.  However, such effects are expected to
be significant only if the GBT beam is pointed at a region with strong sources,
such as the galactic plane.  For pointings in regions of low signal, even much
larger changes in the definition of sidelobe-vs.-beam cutoff have little effect.
Effects of uncertainties in the horizon should be small (it would take a uniform
0.5-degree error in the horizon to yield errors of rms 1%%, max 20%%; largest
effects would be for pointings within a few degrees of the horizon).  The
height-above-ground from which the horizon is calculated will have some effect
(at least for sources near the horizon).  For the GBT, the program allows
horizons from height-above-ground = 50 metres (best) or 150 metres (almost as
good), which appear to have been calculated from topographical maps of the
region [interpolation in horizon-parameters between these might lead to a slight
improvement, but probably would not be worthwhile].  For the 140-foot telescope,
the measured horizon elevation angle (from a platform up on the telescope) is
Refraction has been included, since it can have a non-negligible effect on the
stray radiation contribution even for pointings far above the horizon, largely
due to the fact that more of the sky is visible to the telescope than would be
the case without refraction (roughly, an extra strip about 0.3 degrees wide).
Note that, even with refraction included, computed beam azimuths and elevations
(from RA,DEC or GLON,GLAT) may differ on occasion by almost 0.1 degrees from
the AZIMUTH,ELEVATIO values stored by the GBT software; but this should yield
only small errors in the strayscan (about rms 0.2%%, max 3%%).
Variation of refraction with atmospheric conditions is not too large (perhaps
20%% of the already-small refraction effect, usually; somewhat more for ambient
temperatures above 20 C).  Thus, if pressure, ambient temperature, and relative
humidity (or dew point) are not available from the scan or from a user-supplied
ambient-conditions-file, the program uses default values for these, which should
yield significantly better results than ignoring refraction completely (errors
probably of order rms 0.2%%, max 3%%).  At least as of early 2007, SDFITS files
from the GBT contain the required pressure, temperature, and humidity values.
For earlier GBT files that do not contain them, the 'getfitsambient' script may
be used to extract these data from the GBT archive disks.
The position of the horizon is almost unaffected by refraction (if the horizon
is at a distance of 10 km, refraction typically shifts its position by only
about 0.02 degrees).  One would need to know the distance to (and/or altitude
of) the horizon to correct for this, which information is indeed available for
the GBT, but not for the 140-foot telescope.  The program ignores any horizon
shift, as of the current version; expected errors from this should be negligible
(probably of order rms 0.1%%, max 2%%).
NOTE THAT, while the velocity offset of a scan relative to the Local Standard
of Rest is essentially constant during any reasonable integration time, this
may NOT be the case for the POSITION in galactic coordinates of some point in
the GBT sidelobe, since it is fixed in az-el, and thus rotates on the sky
(rather than being fixed in RA-DEC, like the 140-foot telescope, which thus has
no such problem).  For both telescopes, it is also possible that the sidelobe
position at some galactic position (l,b) may cross the horizon during the
integration time of the scan.  These possibilities have been considered in the
algorithms used here (there is a slight cost in CPU-time if the telescope's
integration time at a single point exceeds 120 sec).
Position of the GBT at Green Banks:
The program uses a (geodetic) GBT latitude of 38.433129 degrees (North) and a
longitude of -79.839840 degrees (negative because it is West), at an elevation
of 824.363 metres (above the ellipsoid), as per the information from
         http://www.gb.nrao.edu/~fghigo/gbtdoc/vlbinfo.html
   The geodetic position for the GBT (as of Jan 2000), based on a local survey
referred to a standard NGS survey marker on the Green Bank site:
  * (NAD83) 79deg 50' 23.406" W, 38deg 25' 59.236" N (+/-0.04")
  * Height of Track: NAVD88 height: 807.43 m (wrt ellipsoid: 776.34 m)
  * Height of elevation axle: NAVD88 height: 855.65 m (wrt ellipsoid: 824.55 m)
The surveyed height refers to the top of the azimuth track. The phase center
(intersection of azimuth and elevation axes) is 48.22m above the top of the
azimuth track. The average geoid height = -31.10 meters with respect to the
   Our best estimate of the ITRF97 earth-centered coordinates for the phase
center of the GBT are derived from analysis of a "TIES" VLBI run with the GBT
and GB 20-meter (December 2002).

    X     (mm)     882589641.08     1.7 
    Y     (mm)   -4924872324.81     6.9
    Z     (mm)    3943729358.48     5.4
    X vel (mm/yr)        -15.34      .1
    Y vel (mm/yr)         -1.38      .2
    Z vel (mm/yr)          3.51      .2
[Reference: ftp://gemini.gsfc.nasa.gov/pub/solutions/2004bn_may04/

The corresponding NAD83 position (and best geodetic position to use) is:
     Latitude  = 38deg 25' 59.266" N              (  38.433129 )
     Longitude = 79deg 50' 23.423" W              ( -79.839840 )
     Height    = 824.363 m (above the ellipsoid)
     Height    = 855.46  m (above the geoid)
Rlocal + h = 6370.74039207 km, r = 5003.33207875 km, z = 3943.72935848 km
Geocentric latitude = 38.245902 = 38deg 14' 45.25"
   NOTE "... radius of the earth 6,378,137 m ..."
from  http://www.gb.nrao.edu/ovlbi/es70_stationLocation.txt
- For zero elevation, tan(geocentric lat.) = (1-f)^2 * tan(geodetic lat.)
where f = 1 / 298.25 = 0.00335289 = Earth's flattening factor
- Note that the geodetic latitude corresponds to the angle of the zenith for an
ellipsoidal model of the Earth relative to the equatorial plane; due to polar
flattening [Rpole=(1-f)*Requator] such a line to the zenith does not pass
through the center of the earth, unlike the definition of geocentric latitude.
- Map coordinates are given as longitude and geodetic latitude.
- Astronomical latitude is the angle between the equatorial plane and the normal
to the geoid (i.e., a plumb line); the geodetic latitude should be close to this
(good enough for our purposes, at the very least); likewise, the astronomical
longitude should be close to the geodetic longitude.
- Geocentric latitude is appropriate for velocity due to Earth's rotation, but
use of geodetic latitude leads to negligible errors (of order 0.001 km/s).
- Astronomical (or Geodetic) latitude is appropriate for getting azimuth and
elevation angles relative to fixed coordinates such as (RA,DEC) or (l,b).
- The telescope elevation is only used when computing refraction corrections.
Position of the 140-foot telescope at Green Banks:
==================================================
The program uses a (geodetic) 140-foot-telescope latitude of 38.4376136 degrees
(North) and a longitude of -79.84167 degrees (i.e., West), at an elevation of
836.0 meters.  Note that this 140-foot longitude is not very accurate (it should
be to the north-east of the GBT, not westwards of it).  [In the original version
of this program, the latitude had been rounded to 38.43 degrees.]
NOTE: actual geocentric latitude = 38 deg 15' 01".28 = 38.250356
        actual geodetic latitude = 38 deg 26' 15".409 = 38.4376136  (used).
Program to compute contribution of sidelobes.
For the HI-survey cube-file name,

the directory to be searched for the above filenames

 variables are all ignored if the

...Usage of the sidelobe program:
%s --help           Briefly describe the usual arguments.
%s --help [OPTION]  Describe the given option & related ones.
%s --help opt       Briefly list the most useful arguments.
%s --help test      Also list less-useful/test arguments.
%s --help env       Describe relevant environment variables.
%s --help main      Describe the most useful arguments.
%s --help use       Describe other useful arguments.
%s --help all       Describe ALL arguments (some briefly).
%s --help err       Discuss sources of error in strayscan.
%s --help pos       Discuss the telescope positions.
%s --help full      Describe EVERYTHING in full detail.
***** sidelobe: COMMAND-LINE PARAMETER ERROR *****
%s [ARGUMENTS]      Run sidelobe with given command-line arguments.
TYPICAL USAGE:  %s [OPTIONS] <inputScanFilename> <outputScanFilename>
OR:  %s [OPTIONS] --in <inputScanFilename> --out <outputScanFilename>
Create a sidelobe correction ("strayscan") output file, of the same type as the
input file (whether SDD-file or SDFITS file), by convolving the telescope's
sidelobe pattern with an all-sky HI survey.  [Note that, for each output
strayscan, the string "STRAY" is appended to the OBJECT name, and in a FITS
file the value of the variable IFNUM is set to 99.]
Brief summaries of some useful options:
  --overwrite : overwrite any existing output scan-file.
  --append : if output scan-file exists, keep it and append new scans to it.
  --correct-stray : instead of outputting the strayscans, subtract the computed
      strayscans from the input (at each point), renormalize, and output the
      resulting stray-corrected spectra.  Note that the string "STCOR" will
      be appended to the OBJECT name of each output stray-corrected spectrum,
      and for a FITS file the variable IFNUM will be incremented by 100.
  --out-stray <strayOutputFilename> : if '--correct-stray' was specified, also
      output the calculated strayscans (to this file).
  --in-stray <strayInputFilename> : alternatively, the previously-computed
      strayscans in this file will be used to obtain the stray-corrected
      output spectra (this option implies '--correct-stray').
  --in-cal <FinCAL> : provide a calibration factor (e.g., 1.08) to multiply the
      input spectra as they are read in (optionally, you can also supply a
      STOKES value, plus a different calibration factor to apply to spectra
      having this STOKES value).
  --force : force the calculation of strayscans for input spectra that would
      normally be ignored (e.g., if the input file already contains strayscans
      or stray-subtracted spectra).
  --GBT : produce sidelobe corrections for the GBT at NRAO (the default is to
      look for the telescope name in the input scan-file).
  --140 : produce sidelobe corrections for the NRAO 140-foot (43 m) Telescope.
  --log-min : minimize log-output to the terminal (standard output), listing
      only scan-number and index-position in file for each scan processed (the
      default is to list several summary lines per scan).
  --quiet : absolute minimum log output: only a few lines of initial and final
      status information, with no listing of scans processed.
  --list : summarize to the terminal (but do not actually process) the scans in
      the input file (no output file should be provided); for example,
      '%s --list --log-min gbt_scan_file.fits'
...Description of all available options:
[To see other options and other modes of operation, use the HELP options above.]
NOTATION:  '[  ]' indicates that a default exists (while [--opt] indicates that
the default is '--opt');  '--optA | --optB' indicates options that are mutually
exclusive;  '-D/--optD' indicates synonyms '-D' and '--optD';  '<value>' stands
for a numeric or character argument that must follow the option.

Meaning of arguments and options (note that most have alternate versions):
  --in <inputScanFilename>  (or -s ... or --scan-input-filename ...):
provide the name of the input scan-file, whether SDD-file or SDFITS-file (with
this '--in' option, the input file can appear anywhere among the arguments: it
need not be the second-last one).  NOTE that the GBT software produces FITS
files in the SDFITS format (SDD-files are from the old 140-foot).
  --out <outputScanFilename>  (or -S ... or --scan-output-filename ...):
provide the name of the output scan-file, whether SDD-file or SDFITS-file (with
this '--out' option, the output file need not be the last of the arguments).
This output scan-file will be of the same type as the input scan-file, and will
generally hold calculated stray radiation spectra ("strayscans").
[NOTE: if the above '--in' ('-s') and '--out' ('-S') options are not used to
specify the scan-files, then the last two command-line arguments are in general
interpreted as <inputScanFilename> and <outputScanFilename>.]
  --keep  (or -k or --append or --append-to-output-file): if the output
scan-file already exists, append new scans to it (better to AVOID this).
  --delete  (or -d or --overwrite or --overwrite-output-file): if the output
scan-file already exists, overwrite it.
[It is a fatal error if the output scan-file already exists, but you have not
specified '--append', '--overwrite', or '--interactive': for the last of these,
the choice to append or overwrite will be prompted for.]
  --nscans <Nscans>  (or -n ... or --number-of-scans-in-output-sdd-file ...):
specify the maximum number of scans that a newly-created output SDD-file should
be able to hold; irrelevant for the GBT (do '--help --nscans' for details).
be able to hold (the default is the number of scans in the input SDD-file,
rounded up to a multiple of 8; a negative input value yields this default, but
any non-negative input value less than 512 will yield a value of 1024).  This
option affects ONLY the older SDD-files, not SDFITS-files created by the GBT.
  --verify-checksum  (or --verify-fitsio-CHECKSUM-in-FITS-files): for any FITS
file that is being read in, if the 'CHECKSUM' and/or 'DATASUM' keywords are
present but have incorrect values, stop with a fatal error.  
internal checksums, which can be written and checked via the subroutines of the
'fitsio' FITS file manipulation package, are neither written (if absent) nor
updated (if present) by many other FITS file packages.  Thus, by default,
absence of these keywords is ignored, and bad values just yield a warning.]
  --require-checksum  (or --require-fitsio-CHECKSUM-in-FITS-files): Similar to
the option '--verify-checksum' above, except that it is also a fatal error if
the 'CHECKSUM' or 'DATASUM' keyword is missing from an input FITS file.
  --correct-stray  (or -E or --correct-input-spectra-for-stray-radiation
or --subtract-stray): the output file will contain the input spectra corrected
for stray radiation, i.e., with stray radiation subtracted (rather than the
strayscans themselves).   The output spectra then are given by
   out = ( FCAL * in - stray ) / ( 1.0 - WTOTSDLB ),
where FCAL is an optional user-specified calibration factor (see '--in-cal'),
and WTOTSDLB is the fraction of the weight of the telescope beam that comes
from the "sidelobes" [i.e., the fraction coming from beyond <zeroNearRadius>
from the beam: see the options '--zero-near' and '--zero-far'].
Roughly, for the GBT,  out = ( FCAL * in - stray ) / ( 1 - 0.12 ),
and for the 140-foot,  out = ( FCAL * in - stray ) / ( 1 - 0.162 );
[NOTE that, for the GBT, if the input and stray files have data units 'Ta*',
this implies that they have been corrected for atmospheric attenuation; if they
have units 'Ta', this implies that they have not been corrected for atmospheric
attenuation (note that this is a function of the beam elevation angle).  The
sidelobe program will correctly handle any case where one has been corrected
and the other has not.  ALSO: instead of computing the strayscans to use in the
stray radiation correction, these can be read in from previously-computed
strayscan file: see the option '--in-stray' below.]
  --sub-mul <SMUL>  (or -F ... or --subtract-strayscans-multiplied-by-factor
...): TEST CASE: similar, with modified sidelobe normalization (DO NOT USE THIS).
...): TEST CASE: this is the same as '--correct-stray' above, except that the
overall magnitude of the sidelobes are multiplied by <SMUL> (i.e., the stray
spectra and the value of <Sint> are both multiplied by <SMUL> in the above
formulae; note that <SMUL> must not exceed 3.0).  DO NOT USE THIS OPTION.
  --in-stray <strayInputFilename>  (or -j ... or --input-stray-file-name ...
or --stray-file-to-use-to-correct-input-spectra ...): instread of calculating
the required strayscans for stray-correction, use the ones contained in the
file <strayInputFilename>, which must have been previously computed from the
input file that is being corrected here.  (Note that this '--in-stray' option
also implies '--correct-stray'.  The strayscans from this file will be checked
to ensure that they actually correspond to the input spectra.)
  --out-stray <strayOutputFilename>  (or --strayscan-also-output-filename ...
or -D ...): if you specify '--correct-stray' (as described above), then this
option can be used to cause the calculated strayscans to be output as well,
to the file <strayOutputFilename> (the stray-subtracted spectra go to the usual
output file).  [Note that if you specify '--append' or '--overwrite', then they
apply to this stray output file as well as to the usual output file.]
  --from-vac  (or --input-scan-was-corrected-for-attenuation): assume that the
input spectra had already been corrected for atmospheric attenuation (by the
GBT software that produced that input file), if this fact cannot be determined
from the input file itself (i.e., if the input-file data units are neither 'Ta*'
nor 'Ta').  [Note: the input-file data units are overridden and the data units
reset to 'Ta*' if you also specify '--reset-units'.]
  --from-atm  (or -h or --input-scan-not-corrected-for-attenuation): assume
that the input spectrum had NOT been corrected for atmospheric attenuation.
[Again, '--reset-units' overrides the input and sets data units to 'Ta'.]
  --to-vac  (or --output-scan-should-be-corrected-for-attenuation): the computed
output strayscans (and also the stray-subtracted output scans, if you specify
the '--correct-stray' option) are corrected for atmospheric attenuation (i.e.,
the spectra are corrected to their vacuum values).  This is the DEFAULT case.
  --to-atm  (or -H or --output-scan-not-corrected-for-attenuation): the computed
output strayscans (and/or the stray-subtracted output scans) are NOT corrected
  --in-cal <FinCAL> <STOKESin> <FinSTOKES>  (or --input-file-calibration ...
or --calibration-factor-for-input-scan-file ...):
by <FinCAL> as they are read in (or by <FinSTOKES>, if the Stokes value matches
the character or integer value <STOKESin>).  [Do '--help --in-cal' for details.]
input file by the calibration factor <FinCAL> as they are read in [an input
value of -1.0E+30 (or more negative) will be reset to the DEFAULT calibration
factor of unity].  If a STOKES value <STOKESin> is specified, then any spectra
with that STOKES value will by multiplied by <FinSTOKES> (instead of <FinCAL>).
[Character STOKES values I, Q, U, V,  RR, LL, RL, LR, XX, YY, XY, YX can be used
instead of corresponding integers 1, 2, 3, 4,  -1, -2, -3, -4, -5, -6, -7, -8;
note that specifying <STOKESin> = 0 or -9 is the same as omitting it, i.e., in
this case <FinCAL> would be applied to all input-file spectra.]  While <FinCAL>
(and <FinSTOKES>) will usually be close to unity, and a warning message is given
for values outside the range [ 0.8 : 1.2 ], nonetheless any value in the range
[ -1.0E+20 : 1.0E+20 ] will be accepted and used (including zero).  [NOTE: this
input-file calibration factor is irrelevant to the calculation of strayscans,
but it WILL affect output files resulting from '--correct-stray', or from any
versions of the '--extract', '--stack', '--cmp', or '--plot' options.  It will
NOT be applied to any compare-file (from the '--cmp' options), but it WILL be
applied to any file specified by the '--stack-file' option, unless an individual
calibration factor is explicitly specified for that file.]
  --def  (or --ok or --default-speed-and-accuracy): calculate the sidelobe
corrections quickly and accurately (about 0.3 seconds per scan for a 2-GHz CPU;
rms error of 0.2%% and maximum expected error of 2%% in regions where sidelobe
correction exceeds 0.1 K, i.e., comparable to basic uncertainty from the 1-km/s
velocity resolution of the spectra, and probably much smaller than the errors
in the telescope-beam sidelobe pattern
).  This option is the DEFAULT case.
(To see the equivalent individual "test" options, do '--help --def'.)
  --fast  (or --faster-but-less-accurate): this is roughly a factor of two
faster than the default case, but about five times less accurate
  --acc  (or --slightly-more-accurate-but-slower): this is about a factor of two
slower than the default case, but does not increase the basic uncertainties
(i.e., slightly more accurate than the default case ['--def']
; this option is equivalent to specifying
'--domega-def --v-lin --lin-side --good-precess --levels 0 %d %d
 --v-comb %d %d %d %d --deltas %.3f %.3f %.3f %.3f --cut %.2f %d %d %.3f
 --end-trim-avg --trim-slope %.2f --v-lim %.1f %.1f 9999.0 -9999.0
 --time-limit %.1f %.1f %d %.1e --refract').
This option is the DEFAULT case, and can be omitted (unless you wish to
override defaults that may be set by the HI-survey cube-file).
 --time-limit %.1f %.1f %d %.1e',
plus '--refract' [for GBT] or '--refract-approx' [for 140-foot telescope]).
  --v-convol <vFWHM>  (or --convolve-input-gaussian-velocity-FWHM-kms ...):
for the input SDFITS or SDD file, convolve all the input spectra, as they are
read in, with a gaussian of the specified FWMH in km/s [zero yields the default
of no convolution; negative or missing input yields <vFWHM> = %.3f km/s (which
will convert a resolution of %.3f to %.3f km/s)].  Note that no other files
be affected; only the input-file spectra will be convolved.
  --to-LSR  (or --to-lsr or --convert-input-scan-spectrum-frame-to-LSR): For an
SDFITS input file: as each scan is read in, the spectrum reference frame will
be converted to the Local Standard of Rest (LSR) if it is not already in that
frame.  ANY output files produced will thus have spectra in the LSR frame.
[Cannot be used with old SDD-files.]  (For more details, do '--help --to-LSR'.)
Appropriate changes will be made in the values of CTYPEn, CRVALn, and CDELTn
(where n = the velocity- or frequency-axis index); if SPECSYS is present, it
will be reset to 'LSR', and if VELOSYS is present, it will be reset to zero.
If present, OBSFREQ and FREQRES will be multiplied by the appropriate Doppler
factors (note that RESTFREQ, RESTFRQ, VELDEF, and VFRAME will not be changed).
If present, RVSYS will be shifted, but POSSIBLY IN THE WRONG DIRECTION (for
example, if VELDEF = 'RADI-LSR' and the input spectrum was in the 'OBS' frame,
the value of VFRAME will be added to RVSYS; it is not clear from the definition
of RVSYS whether VFRAME should perhaps be subtracted instead).  Note that an
input 'VELO-OBS' axis will be shifted non-relativistically by the velocity
difference between telescope and LSR frames; but for a 'FREQ-OBS' or 'FELO-OBS'
axis, the relativistic Doppler shift (from this velocity difference) will be
applied to the relevant axis parameters.  (See also '--calc-v-LSR'.)
  --set-stokes  (or --set-stokes-I): in the output (strayscan) SDFITS file, set
the STOKES-axis value to unity, i.e., Stokes I (the DEFAULT is to leave this
unchanged unless one is stacking multiple spectra [see '--stack']).
  --keep-stokes  (or --no-set-stokes-I): in the output SDFITS file, keep the
STOKES value obtained from the input file, even if one is stacking spectra.
  --list  (or -L or --list-scans-from-input-file): do not process any scans,
but summarize to the terminal (standard output) scans from the input scan-file.
(No output scan-file name should be specified.)
[AVOID the options '--max-out' and '--more-out' (these are only available for
compatibility with older program versions; do '--help --max-out' for details).]
  --max-out  (or --maximum-scan-output-level): for the output SDD-file, write
all possible scans, i.e., the strayscan, the processed scan, any copy-scan, the
nearscan, and any component scans (default is to output only the strayscan).
Kept for compatibility with old program versions; NOT RECOMMENDED.
  --more-out <outScans>  (or --more-scan-output-level ...): for each processed
input scan, <outScans> gives the number of scans that will be added to the
output SDD-file: '--more-out 0' corresponds to '--list', while '--more-out 1'
corresponds to the default case (strayscan only).  AVOID THIS OPTION.
  --force  (or --force-calculation-of-stray-spectra): Normally, stray radiation
spectra are neither calculated nor output for input scans that appear to be
stray spectra (or stray-subtracted spectra) already.  This option forces the
calculation and output of strayscans even for such input spectra.
  --quiet  (or -q): send only a bit of initial and final log-output to the
terminal (i.e., to standard output), with NO list of scans processed or ignored
  --just-head  (or -j or --log-just-header-lines): like '--quiet' above, except
that the full set of program-startup "header" lines are output to the terminal,
describing what the program will do (equivalent to '--quiet --log-with-head').
  --log-overwrite  (or -U or --overwrite-strayscan-status-line-on-screen):
for each strayscan computed (equivalent to '--log -1').
[Do not use this when redirecting the standard output to a file.]
  --log-min  (or -u or --log-only-scan-number-and-index): minimal log-output:
print the scan number and its index-position in the input scan-file for each
scan processed, listing %d scans per output line
[Note that if all spectrum data are NULL ("UNDEFINED"), this will be indicated.]
  --log-short  (or ---log-scan-number-index-and-object): short log-output:
print the scan number, its index-position in the input scan-file, and its object
name for each scan processed (if you also specify '--log-wide' [see below], then
the RA,DEC position is output too).  If you are just listing an input file
(see '--list'), this yields one line of output per scan; if the strayscan is
being computed, another line is output to summarize this computation.
  --log-def  (or --log-important-scan-parameters): output several lines of
description for each scan that is or would be processed.  This output includes
the RA,DEC position is output too) [equivalent to '--log 1'].  If just listing
an input file (see '--list'), this yields one line of output per scan; if the
strayscan is being computed, a second line is output to summarize it.
description for each scan processed (equivalent to '--log %d'): this outputs
the scan number, the index-position '@' in the file, position 'RA' in hr:min:sec
and 'DEC' in deg:min:sec, 'LST' in hr:min:sec, duration 'dt' in sec, exposure
'ex' in sec [if different], time 'UT' and date, telescope azimuth and elevation
'az,el' in deg, galactic coordinates 'l,b' in deg, and velocity-range in km/s;
for the GBT, the velocity-range is preceded by the data units ('Ta*' for spectra
corrected for atmospheric absorption, 'Ta' for uncorrected spectra, in general).
If a strayscan is being calculated, some more lines are output to summarize it.
[NOTE: for the GBT, the scan number is not a unique identifier, and is output as
  SCAN[INDEX_IN_SCAN]STOKES  where SCAN is the scan number, INDEX_IN_SCAN counts
the number of sky-positions encountered having scan-number SCAN, and STOKES is
the Stokes parameter.  (Use of the range-selection option '--index' may affect
the value of INDEX_IN_SCAN, since not all of the spectra for a given SCAN may
be encountered.)  If STOKES is unavailable or repeated, it is replaced by an
index that counts the number of spectra encountered at that sky position.]
This is the DEFAULT log case, so this option usually need not be specified.
(See also '--log-def-after' and '--v-log-lsr'.)
  --log-full  (or -W or --log-all-scan-parameters): output all known information
for each scan processed, except for the spectrum
  --log-max  (or --log-all-scan-parameters-plus-spectrum): output all known
information for each scan processed, including all of the spectrum values
  --log-wide  (or -w or --wide or --very-long-log-lines): for log-output to the
terminal, allow long line lengths, up to about 250 chars.  [With '--log-def'
(see above), listing an input file yields only one of these long output lines
per scan, but a couple more lines are output when computing strayscans.]
[AVOID the general case '--log <logLevel>' ('-Q ...'): it is complicated (you
can do '--help --log' if you need all the details).]
 (equivalent to '--log 9995').
(equivalent to '--log 999999995').
  --log <logLevel>  (or -Q ... or --log-level ...): general case of the above
log-output options: <logLevel> is an integer flag that determines how much
log-output to the terminal (i.e., to the standard output) is produced.  An
input value of -9 or less means 'use the DEFAULT value of %d' (while -2 yields
'--quiet', and -1 yields '--log-overwrite').  Otherwise, the given <logLevel>
value is decomposed into three integer flags I, CCC, and DDDDD, such that
<logLevel> = DDDDDCCCI = 10000 * DDDDD + 10 * CCC + I, where these flags have
     I = 0 : just output the scan-number and the index-position in the file;
               in this case only, up to %d scans per line may be output.
     I = 1 : output scan-number, index-position in file, and object name
               (if a strayscan is being calculated, also output a line with the
               total sidelobe weight and number of sidelobe points used).
     I = 2 : also output RA (hour:min:sec), DEC (deg:min:sec) on next line.
     I = 3 : also output the epoch of the RA,DEC values (1950 or 2000).
     I = 4 : on next line, also output LST, duration 'dt', UT, and date; and also
               exposure 'ex', if it differs from duration.
     I = 5 : also output azimuth, elevation, and (on next line) the galactic
               coordinates l,b and velocity range v_lo:v_hi
               [if a strayscan is being calculated, the ambient temperature,
               pressure, and humidity are output, plus the azimuth, elevation
               and observed elevation (due to refraction) of the beam at
               mid-duration point(s), plus a breakdown (by tile-size) of
               contributions to the sidelobe weight (on several more lines)].
     I = 6 to 9 : same as 2 to 5, except that two or three lines are combined
                    into each longer line (usually less than 255 characters).
       0 < CCC < 999 : SDFITS table column CCC is output (unless it is DATA).
       CCC = 999 : all SDFITS table columns/keywords are output (except DATA).
       0 < CCC < 14 : output the variables in scan header class 'CCC'.
       CCC = 100 : output the scan preamble.
       CCC > 100 : output preamble and all scan header classes up to (CCC-100).
     DDDDD > 0 : output up to DDDDD data points (spectrum values) from the scan.
  --log-with-head  (or --with-head or --log-with-header-lines): the full set of
program-startup "header" lines are output to the terminal, describing what the
program will do (this is the default case, unless you have specified '--quiet').
  --log-no-head  (or --no-head or --log-with-no-header-lines):  very little of
the program-startup log output is produced (this is the default for '--quiet').
  --v-log-lsr  (or --shift-velocity-to-LSR-in-list-output): when the scan's
velocity range is output to the terminal, do so in the LSR velocity frame
(rather than the velocity frame used in the scan itself).
  --quiet-after <Nq>  (or --quiet-after-Nscans ...): after printing summaries
for <Nq> scans, stop doing so, as if '--quiet' had been specified at that point.
If <Nq> is negative, do the switch  | <Nq> |  scans before the end of the input
file; if <Nq> is zero, no such switch will occur.
  --log-min-after <Nmin>  (or --log-minimal-after-Nscans ...): similar, but
switch to minimal output at the specified point.
  --log-def-after <Ndef>  (or --log-default-after-Nscans ...): similar, but
switch to default output at the specified point.  For example, specifying
'--quiet-after 2 --log-def-after -2' would generally summarize to the terminal
the first two and the last two scans in the input file, while specifying
'--index-range 11 20 --quiet --log-def-after 2 --quiet-after 4' would summarize
to the terminal the 13th and 14th scans in the input file.
  --index-range <INDEXlo> <INDEXhi>  (or -r ... or --scan-index-range ...):
allowed range of index-positions in input scan file (DEFAULT is <INDEXlo> = 1,
<INDEXhi> = %d, i.e., no restriction): this index is the position in an
SDD-file, or the row number in an SDFITS-file from the GBT.  Note that a
negative input value refers to an index relative to the end of the file (i.e.,
-1 refers to the last spectrum in the file, -2 to the next-to-last, etc.),
while an input value of zero is reset to its corresponding default value.
  --scan-range <SCANlo> <SCANhi>  (or -R ... or --scan-number-range ...):
allowed range of scan-numbers for input scans (DEFAULT is <SCANlo> = 0.00,
<SCANhi> = %.2f, i.e., no restriction): this scan-number is the 'SCAN'
value in the input file.  In an SDFITS-file, the SCAN number may not be unique,
and may be an integer (for purposes of the sidelobe program, 0.01 will be added
to an integer SCAN number [or 0.51 for an identifiable strayscan, 0.61 for an
identifiable stray-subtracted spectrum], but values in files remain integers).
  --scan-frac <fracSCANlo> <fracSCANhi>  (or -f ... or --scan-fraction-range
...): allowed range of the 2-digit fractional part of the scan-number (DEFAULT
is <fracSCANlo> = .00, <fracSCANhi> = .995, i.e., no restriction).  This option
is intended for use with SDD-files; AVOID using with SDFITS-files from the GBT.
  --stokes-range <STOKESlo> <STOKEShi>  (or --SDFITS-STOKES-range ...): for an
SDFITS input file ONLY: allowed range of the STOKES parameter (note that the
character STOKES values I, Q, U, V,  RR, LL, RL, LR, XX, YY, XY, YX can be used
instead of corresponding integers 1, 2, 3, 4,  -1, -2, -3, -4, -5, -6, -7, -8
when specifying <STOKESlo> or <STOKEShi>).  Note that if the SDFITS file does
not specify the STOKES parameter, it is assumed to be I (i.e., 1).
  --l-range <galLONGlo> <galLONGhi>  (or --galactic-longitude-range-in-degrees
... or --gal-long-range ...): only process scans lying inside the given galactic
longitude range in degrees; the two values must be between -360 and +720 deg,
and their difference must be less than 360 deg (if <galLONGlo> is larger than
<galLONGhi>, then, in effect, the selected range is from <galLONGlo> up to
<galLONGhi> + 360 , or, equivalently, from  <galLONGlo> - 360  to <galLONGhi>).
Inputs in 'ddd:mm:ss.s' format are allowed, as well as in decimal degrees.
  --b-range <galLATlo> <galLAThi>  (or --galactic-latitude-range-in-degrees ...
or --gal-lat-range ...): only process scans in the given galactic latitude range
in degrees (large positive or negative values mean 'no limit at this end of the
range'; and if <galLATlo> is larger than <galLAThi>, only process scans from
<galLATlo> to 90 deg and from -90 to <galLAThi> deg).  Inputs in 'ddd:mm:ss.s'
  --ra-range <RAlo> <RAhi>  (or --right-ascension-range-in-hours ...
or --RA-range ...): only process scans in the given right-ascension (RA) range
in hours; the two values must be between -24 and +48 hours, and their difference
must be less than 24 hours (if <RAlo> is larger than <RAhi>, the allowed range
is the 'other way' around the circle, as for the '--l-range' options above).
Inputs in 'hh:mm:ss.s' format are allowed, as well as in decimal hours.
  --dec-range <DEClo> <DEChi>  (or --declination-range-in-degrees ...
or --DEC-range ...): only process scans in the given declination (DEC) range in
degrees (large positive or negative values mean 'no limit at this end of range';
and if <DEClo> is larger than <DEChi>, only process scans from <DEClo> to 90 deg
and from -90 to <DEChi> deg).  Inputs in 'ddd:mm:ss.s' format are allowed, as
  --object-range <OBJECTlo> <OBJECThi>  (or --object-name-alphabetic-range ...
or -J ...): process only scans with OBJECT names in the alphabetic range from
'<OBJECTlo>' to '<OBJECThi>', inclusive.  NOTE that for an input string of
length N, only the first N characters of the OBJECT name will be checked; the
maximum allowed input string-length is N = %d.
  --object-keep <OBJECTkeep>  (or -o ... or --object-name-to-include ...):
process only scans with OBJECT names that begin with the string '<OBJECTkeep>'
(this option may be given up to %d times, allowing one to simultaneously select
several different objects).  [This option is OR'd with option '--object-range'.]
  --not-object <OBJECTexclude>  (or -O ... or --object-name-to-exclude ...):
process only scans with OBJECT names that do NOT begin with the given string
'<OBJECTexclude>' (this option may be given up to %d times).
[Scans that lie outside ANY of the above ranges will ALWAYS be silently ignored
(i.e., the different types of ranges above are AND'ed together).]
  --GBT  (or -g or --GBT-telescope): calculate the sidelobe contribution for
spectra from the GBT at NRAO (almost identical to '--GBT-50', except for using
an elevation of %.3f m, from 'phase-center height above geoid').
  --GBT-50  (or --GBT-telescope-horizon-from-50-metres-up): calculate sidelobe
contribution for the GBT at NRAO, but set the elevation to (and use the horizon
as seen from) a point 50 metres above the ground-level (or 806 m above geoid).
  --GBT-150  (or --GBT-telescope-horizon-from-150-metres-up): like '--GBT-50',
but the elevation/horizon-viewpoint is 150 metres above ground level.
  --GBT-02  (or --GBT-telescope-horizon-from-2-metres-up): like '--GBT-50', but
the elevation/horizon-viewpoint is 2 metres above the ground.  NOT RECOMMENDED.
  --GBT-ground  (or --GBT-telescope-ground-level-horizon): TEST CASE: calculate
sidelobe contribution for the GBT at NRAO, but use the old estimate of the GBT
horizon, as obtained from an early ground level measurement (and also the old
elevation estimate of %.3f m).  NOT RECOMMENDED.
  --140  (or -T or --140-foot-telescope-at-NRAO): calculate the sidelobe
contribution for spectra from the NRAO 140-Foot Telescope.
[AVOID '--140-orig' and '--orig-method' (present only for compatibility with
the original 140-foot program version; do '--help --140-orig' for details).]
  --140-orig  (or --140-foot-telescope-original-version): calculate the
sidelobe contribution for the NRAO 140-Foot Telescope, using all of the
original defaults, including a less-good version of the sidelobe formula, the
incorrect atmospheric extinction formula, and ignoring refraction (this is
equivalent to specifying '--140 --max-out --orig-side --domega-orig --use-rvsys
 --v-spline --smooth-horizon 0.0 --v-comb 1 1 1 1 --cut 0.0 --use-lst --def-tau
 --orig-tau --n-tau 0 --time-limit -1.0 -1.0 %d 0.0 --use-start-time
 --v-lim -999.0 999.0 9999.0 -9999.0 --zero-near %.1f %.1f --zero-far %.1f
 --no-refract --orig-lat --orig-precess --side-no-mul --cube %s').
  --orig-method  (or --original-cube-file-and-algorithm): TEST CASE: use the
original algorithm, with NO tiling of the galactic plane, no refraction, and
parameters as if you had also specified '--140-orig'; this only works with the
OLD binary HI-survey cube-file.  NOT RECOMMENDED.
[If none of these telescope-selection options are given, the program uses the
TELESCOP value from the input file (either a keyword or else a column value in
the first scan in the file), to choose between the cases '--GBT' or '--140'.]
  --aniso <fineHIcubeFilename> <DlbAniso> <vFWHMfine> <WTfine>  (or --aniso-cube
... or --GBT-near-beam-anisotropy-effect-HI-cube-file ...): instead of computing
sidelobes, either manipulate this specifed input FITS HI cube, or else use it
to estimate the effect of the anisotropy of the GBT near beam
  --fine-HI <fineHIcubeFilename> <DlbAniso> <vFWHMfine> <WTfine>  (or --fine-hi
... or --fine-grid-HI-cube-input-file ...): this is almost the same as '--aniso'
above (for details, do '--help --fine-HI').
  --fine-cut <vLO> <vHI>  (or --fine-HI-cube-cut-and-interpolate-v-range-kms ...
or --fine-v-cut ... or --fine-HI-v-cut ...): when reading in any cube specified
by '--aniso' or '--fine-HI' above, cut out the region from <vLO> to <vHI> (km/s
in LSR) and replace it by interpolation from its endpoints in all spectra (this
option can be given multiple times, to specify multiple v-ranges to cut out).
  --mem-aniso <limGb>  (or --memory-limit-in-Gb-for-fine-HI-cubes ...
or --mem-fine-HI ...): reset the total allowed memory allocation limit for the
above HI cubes (default of %.2f Gb; do '--help --mem-aniso' for details).
  --aniso-lin  (or --linear-interpolation-in-GBT-anisotropy-HI-cube): for the
above fine-grid HI cubes, use linear interpolation in GLON and GLAT (rather than
the default of bi-quadratic interpolation, which resembles a spline).  This also
applies to (l,b)-interpolation in cubes used for edge-extension in convolutions
(but does NOT affect velocity-interpolation, which is always bi-quadratic when
handling the above fine-grid HI cubes).
[ --aniso-extend <dA> , --aniso-extend-flat <dA> , --aniso-extend-mean <dA> ,
--aniso-extend-beam <dA> : for beam-anisotropy cases where the fine-grid HI cube
does not extend far enough beyond the area covered by the input file, specify
various approximations to extend it (do '--help --aniso-extend' for details). ]
MUST be on either a GLS grid or a Cartesian grid, either in Galactic or RA,DEC
coordinates [it would usually be on a finer grid than the LD-survey (LAB) cube].
The filename may optionally be followed by a numerical-convolution grid-size
<DlbAniso> in arcseconds [should be not more than the HI-cube grid-size (or even
smaller, in some cases); a missing or non-positive value will be reset to the
default of %.1f arcsec]; by <vFWHMfine>, the width in km/s of a gaussian to
convolve the velocity-axis when the cube is input [a missing value yields the
default of 0.0 (i.e., no convolution), while a negative input value will yield
<vFWHMfine> = %.3f km/s (to convert a resolution of %.3f to %.3f km/s)];
and by <WTfine>, a relative weight to use when averaging in regions where cubes
overlap (a missing or non-positive value yields the default of <WTfine> = 1.0).
  ...To estimate the effect of the GBT-beam anisotropy:
You must specify input and output SDFITS files as usual (note that many stray
radiation options, such as '--correct-stray' and '--stray', are not allowed).
This anisotropy calculation is not unlike calculation of a strayscan (with files
that MUST be in SDFITS format), except that the resulting spectra in the output
SDFITS file are the convolutions of the anisotropic part of the GBT near beam
with this HI cube (only the anisotropic GBT beam inside the sidelobe-cutoff
<zeroNearRadius> will be used: see '--zero-near' and '--zero-far').  Note that
this option may be repeated, up to a total of %d times, to specify multiple HI
cubes (but if you specify <DlbAniso>, only the LAST non-default value is used).
Note that you would NOT usually wish to apply any convolution via <vFWHMfine>
(nor would you usually wish to use '--lb-convol' or '--fine-to-car' options).
  ...To just manipulate this input cube (<DlbAniso> is ignored):
You must also specify '--only-read-cube' (or '--only-create-cube'); then this
option '--aniso' will behave like '--fine-HI' below:
above, but (provided you do NOT explicitly specify '--aniso' or a '--make-GLS'
option) you need not specify explicitly (via '--only-read-cube') that you are
just plotting or manipulating a fine-HI cube.  Note that, while <DlbAniso> will
be ignored, any or all fine-HI cube(s) that you specify can have a velocity-axis
convolution performed as they are read in (via their respective <vFWHMfine>
values).  However, the following manipulations will be performed only on the
FIRST fine-HI cube that you specify (and only this possibly-modified fine-HI
cube can be output to a FITS file):
     '--lb-convol <lbFWHM> <Vtol>' convolves in the (l,b)-plane with a gaussian
of FWHM = <lbFWHM> arcseconds; if you specify multiple fine-HI cubes, the other
ones are used to extend the cube edges (for details, do '--help --lb-convol').
     '--fine-to-car' regrids the cube onto a Cartesian grid (the default 0.5x0.5
degree Galactic grid can be changed by '--lat-car', '--long-car', '--v-car', and
'--car-radec'; for details, do '--help --fine-to-car' [see also '--aniso-lin']).
     '--out-cube <outCubeFilename>' writes the possibly-modified fine-HI cube
to a new FITS cube output file with the specified name.
     '--plot-cube' yields text plotfiles for each b (or DEC) value in the cube;
plotfiles will be in the local directory.  Each plotfile name will comprise the
output (or input) FITS filename followed by the b or DEC value being used.
above HI cubes (non-positive input yields the default of %.2f Gb).  At slightly
over 4 bytes per pixel, this memory limit is relevant only if you are inputting
large HI cubes, or very many small ones: for example, even a 500 x 500 x 500
cube requires less than 0.5 Gb of storage.  [Note that this memory allocation
limit applies ONLY to fine-HI cubes specified via the '--aniso' or '--fine-HI'
options.  It does NOT include other arrays used by the program (of order 0.01 Gb
in this case); nor does this limit include space allocated to any GLS-cube being
created (see the '--make-gls' options), nor temporary space required to perform
convolutions (see '--lb-convol') or regridding (see '--fine-to-car'), which
will temporarily allocate enough additional memory to hold the revised cube.]
  --aniso-extend <dA>  (or --continue-beyond-edge-of-GBT-anisotropy-HI-cube
...): only for the case where the GBT near-beam anisotropy is being calculated:
extend the above fine-grid HI cube up to a maximum of <dA> degrees beyond its
(l,b)-edges, using a 'reflection' of its slope (non-positive input yields the
maximum possible extension, i.e., up to the width of the cube).
  --aniso-extend-flat <dA>  (or --extend-constant-edge-of-GBT-anisotropy-HI-cube
...): extend the above fine-grid HI cube up to a maximum of <dA> degrees beyond
its (l,b)-edges, with the same spectra as at the edge.
  --aniso-extend-mean <dA>  (or --extend-average-edge-of-GBT-anisotropy-HI-cube
its (l,b)-edges, using the average spectrum (over the whole cube) there.
  --aniso-extend-beam <dA>  (or --extend-GBT-anisotropy-HI-cube-by-beam-spectrum
its (l,b)-edges, using the spectrum at the position of the GBT beam (if the GBT
beam center lies outside all HI cubes, this behaves like '--aniso-extend-mean').
[Note that even for cases where the fine-grid HI cube does not extend far enough
beyond the area covered by the input file to allow the complete convolution (out
to <zeroNearRadius>), the default (no-extension) case may not always be worse
than one of the above extension methods.]
[ --fine-interp , --fine-interp-gauss : interpolate input fine-HI cube(s) onto
positions from an input SDFITS-file (do '--help --fine-interp' for details). ]
  --fine-interp  (or --interpolate-fine-HI-cube-over-disk-zero-near): this
option modifies the effect of '--aniso': instead of convolving the anisotropic
part of the beam, a 'top-hat' (flat disk) shape is used (yielding an average
over this disk that is modified only by variation in Doppler shifts, refraction,
and atmospheric attenuation, relative to the disk center [which will usually be
fairly small]).  In effect, for each sky-position from the input SDFITS-file,
the spectra from the specified fine-HI cube(s) will be shifted in velocity to
account for the Doppler shift (this shift will vary slightly relative to that
at the beam center, depending also on the date and time [specified in the input
SDFITS file] and on ambient conditions at the telescope [from the input file or
an ambient-conditions file: see '--amb']); the spectra will then be averaged
over a disk of radius <zeroNearRadius> degrees (default %.3f deg: change this
via the option '--zero-near <zeroNearRadius>'); finally, the average spectrum
will be shifted in velocity into the reference frame used in the input SDFITS
file.  Since no telescope has a 'top-hat' beam, this option is useful largely
for tests, UNLESS you set <zeroNearRadius> = 0.0: in this case, no averaging is
performed, and spectra from the fine-HI cube(s) are just interpolated to the
input SDFITS-file positions.  [NOTE, however, that even if the fine-HI cube had
been created from the input SDFITS-file, the output SDFITS-file would differ
from the input, due partly to the fact that two velocity interpolations are
performed, but to an even larger extent to the fact that the input SDFITS-file
positions are rarely at the exact centers of the cube grid-pixels (for example,
210-arcsecond GLS grid scans at the GBT have exhibited position mismatches of up
to 80 arcsec along the scan lines and 25 arcsec perpendicular to them; although
typical mismatches are much smaller, there tends to be a systematic shift of 10
or 20 arcsec along the scan lines, which depends on the direction of the scan,
and thus changes sign for adjacent scan lines).  For such cases, YOU MAY ALSO
NEED TO SPECIFY '--aniso-extend-flat <dA>', with <dA> in degrees being some
fraction of the fine-HI cube grid spacing, to avoid getting NULL spectra for
input SDFITS-file positions whose errors shift them just outside the edges of
the fine-HI cube.]  Multiple fine-HI cubes can of course be specified, and if
<zeroNearRadius> is non-zero, then the grid for the numerical averaging has
angular spacing <DlbAniso> (see '--aniso' or '--fine-HI' above).
  --fine-interp-gauss  (or --interpolate-fine-HI-cube-via-gaussian-zero-near):
this behaves like '--fine-interp', except that the specified fine-HI cube(s)
are convolved with a guassian of FWHM = <zeroNearRadius> degrees that is cut
off (set to zero) at <zeroFarRadius> degrees (default: same as <zeroNearRadius>;
change this via the option '--zero-far <zeroFarRadius>').  The grid for this
numerical convolution (relative to each input/output SDFITS-file position) has
angular spacing <DlbAniso> (see '--aniso' or '--fine-HI' above), with required
spectra interpolated from the fine-HI cubes [in contrast to the '--lb-convol'
option (which uses the fine-HI cube grid for the numerical convolution)].
[ --fine-to-car , --car-radec , --lat-car <NbCar> <DbCar> <bRefCar> ,
--long-car <NlCar> <DlCar> <lRefCar> , --v-car <NvCar> <DvCar> <vRefCar> :
a fine-HI cube, which was either read in from a FITS file (see '--fine-HI') or
else created from an SDFITS file (see the '--make-GLS' options), will be
regridded to Cartesian coordinates; for details, do '--help --fine-to-car'.]
  --fine-to-car  (or --convert-GBT-fine-HI-cube-from-GLS-to-Cartesian
or --GLS-to-car): convert a fine-HI cube (see '--fine-HI', i.e., '--aniso') to
a Galactic Cartesian grid when it is read in; alternatively, if you are creating
a GLS cube from an SDFITS file (see the '--make-GLS' options), do the regridding
before the cube is output.  Bi-quadratic interpolation in (l,b) or (RA,DEC) will
be used unless '--aniso-lin' is specified; a non-default grid may be specified
via '--car-radec', '--lat-car', '--long-car', and '--v-car' below.
  --car-radec  (or --Cartesian-grid-is-in-RA-DEC-coordinate-system): instead of
a Galactic Cartesian grid, convert to a Cartesian grid in RA,DEC (if the input
file is in RA,DEC then its EQUINOX will be used; otherwise, J2000 will be used).
  --lat-car <NbCar> <DbCar> <bRefCar>  (or --b-car ... or --DEC-car ...
or --fine-HI-cube-Cartesian-latitude-grid ...): for the above Cartesian grid,
this allows one to specify a non-default latitude grid (i.e., in b, or in DEC
if you specify '--car-radec').  An <NbCar> value of zero (or -9999) means 'use
as many b/DEC-points as will fit within the farthest extent of the input grid'
(this is the DEFAULT); positive <NbCar> gives an upper limit to the allowed
number of b/DEC-points; negative <NbCar> (but above -9999) means 'use exactly
|<NbCar>| b/DEC-points'.  <DbCar> is the b/DEC-spacing in degrees; missing,
zero, or less than -900 yields the DEFAULT of <DbCar> = %.3f degrees.
<bRefCar> is the b/DEC reference value in degrees (missing, or less than -900,
yields the DEFAULT of a grid that would have a grid-point exactly at a latitude
of zero); also, if <bRefCar> is in the b/DEC-range of the input cube and <NbCar>
is positive, or if <bRefCar> is in the 'allowed' range [-90:90] and <NbCar> is
negative (but larger than -9999), then <bRefCar> is used to specify an edge of
the cube (the lower edge, if <DbCar> is positive; otherwise, the upper edge ---
note that only if <NbCar> is negative can the Cartesian cube reach beyond the
input cube's b/DEC-extent).  [This option implies '--fine-to-car', but does NOT
affect the choice of Galactic vs. RA,DEC coordinate system (see '--car-radec').]
  --long-car <NlCar> <DlCar> <lRefCar>  (or --l-car ... or --RA-car ...
or --fine-HI-cube-Cartesian-longitude-grid ...): for the above Cartesian grid,
specify a non-default longitude grid (in l or in RA).  This behaves similarly
to '--lat-car', EXCEPT that if and only if the input is a GLS cube in the same
coordinate system as the Cartesian cube to be produced, then <NlCar> = -9999
yields a modified default, meaning 'limit the number of longitude points such
that there are no null spectra' (i.e., no spectra lie outside the boundaries of
the input cube); and the 'allowed' range of <lRefCar> is [-360:720] (cf. the
<bRefCar> range [-90:90]).  Note that the DEFAULT <DlCar> = %.3f deg.
  --v-car <NvCar> <DvCar> <vRefCar>  (or --fine-HI-cube-Cartesian-velocity-grid
...): for the above Cartesian cube to be obtained, instead of keeping the input
cube's velocity/frequency axis, interpolate velocities (bi-quadratically) onto
a linear 'VELO-LSR' velocity axis.  Zero or negative <NvCar> means 'use as many
velocity-points as will fit' (this is the DEFAULT); positive <NvCar> gives an
upper limit to the allowed number of velocity-points.  <DvCar> is the velocity
increment in km/s; zero means 'keep the input v-axis', and a missing input (or
an input of less than -900) yields the DEFAULT of <DvCar> = 1.030571969 km/s.
<vRefCar> is the velocity reference value in km/s (a missing input, or input
less than -90000, yields the DEFAULT of a grid that would have a grid-point
exactly at a velocity of zero); also, if <vRefCar> is in the velocity-range of
the input cube and <NvCar> is positive, then <vRefCar> is used to specify a
velocity-axis endpoint (the lower end if <DvCar> is positive, otherwise the
upper end).  [This option implies '--fine-to-car']
  --blank-car <latLO> <longLO> <latHI> <longHI>  (or --blank-car-rect ...
or --blank-Cartesian-rectangle-lat-long-lat-long ...): for the above Cartesian
cube to be obtained, set all of the spectra within the given latitude-longitude
rectangle to 'blank'.  If <latHI> is omitted (or is less than -99), it is set
equal to <latLO>; if <longHI> is omitted (or is less than -999), it is set equal
to <longLO>.  [Note that the cases <latLO> = <latHI> and/or <longLO> = <longHI>
will be widened by half a grid-spacing on each side.]
  --cube <HIsurveyCubeFilename>  (or -c ... or --HI-survey-cube-filename ...):

should be read in (for details, do '--help --cube').

telescope's sidelobes (DEFAULT file name is obtained from environment variable

the observation times (DATE-OBS values) in the input scan-file (such a text file

(time point) in this file consists of either a blank line or a line beginning

temporal order.  An example of a single such entry is as follows:

DATE-OBS= '2005-10-29T00:21:36' / Manager parameter startTime
AMBTEMP =            -1.15E+00 / Ambient Temperature C
AMBPRESS=             9.32E+02 / Ambient Pressure in milliBars
AMBHUMID=             1.00E+00 / Ambient Humidity fraction
conditions from the ambient-conditions file (see the option '--amb' above).
Interpolation is allowed if temporally-adjacent entries are less than <dtInterp>
hours apart (DEFAULT %.1f hours).  Otherwise, the nearest entry will be used,
provided that it is closer to the scan-file observation-time than <dtExtrap>
hours (DEFAULT %.1f hours).  [If neither of these conditions holds, the overall
default ambient conditions will be used (see '--amb-set-def' below).]
be used for scans that do not have values of ambient temperature, pressure, and
relative humidity (or dew point) available [for calculating refraction].
Input value(s) of -99 mean 'use the relevant program default(s)', namely
<TambientDEF> = %.1f degrees Celsius,  <PambientDEF> = %.1f mm Hg,  and
<RelativeHumidityDEF> = %.2f  (note that temperature must be in the range
[%.0f:%.0f], pressure in [%.0f:%.0f], and relative humidity in [0.01:1.00]).
[At least since early 2007 (or perhaps late 2006), GBT SDFITS-files appear to
include ambient condition data, rendering the above three options irrelevant.]
  --extract  (or -e or --extract-spectra-to-output-scan-file): copy the input
spectra to the output file, rather than replacing them with calculated stray
radiation spectra.  [This can create a file containing a subset of the input
spectra (via the options '--index-range', '--scan-range', '--object-range',
'--object-keep', '--not-object', and/or '--stokes-range'), and/or it can correct

if an input-calibration factor was specified (see '--in-cal'), the spectra in
the output file will have been multiplied by this calibration factor.
  --reset-units  (or --reset-DATA-units-atmospheric-correction-flag): assume
that the data units may be erroneous (do '--help --reset-units' for details).
that the data units 'Ta*' or 'Ta' from the input file may be erroneous, i.e.,
that they do not correctly indicate whether the spectram has or has not been
corrected for atmospheric absorption.  The stored data-units value (for each
scan) will be corrected, i.e., reset to 'Ta*' or 'Ta' according to whether the
input parameter '--from-vac' or '--from-atm' has been supplied; if neither of
these was supplied, the value of the 'VACUSDLB' keyword in the file is consulted
(if this keyword exists).  This '--reset-units' option can only be given if you
have also specified '--extract', to correct an erroneous SDFITS file.
  --extract-v-index <vINDXlo> <vINDXhi>  (or --extract-v-index-range ...

range are output (for details, do '--help --extract-v-index').
this behaves like '--extract', but in addition only the part of the spectrum
from <vINDXlo> to <vINDXhi> is extracted.  A non-positive input value of
<vINDXlo> is reset to 1, and a missing or non-positive value of <vINDXhi> (or
a large positive value) is set to the number of velocity-pixels in the first
spectrum read in.  ALL spectra in the input file must have the same number of
pixels, and the output file must not exist, since its binary-table format may
be changed (in particular, the size allocated for DATA storage may change).
  --extract-v-LSR <VloLSR> <VhiLSR>  (or --extract-v-lsr ... or --extract-v ...
or --extract-spectrum-within-LSR-velocity-range ...): For SDFITS files only:

are determined from the velocity pixels between the LSR velocities <VloLSR> and
<VhiLSR> in the first spectrum that is read in (this index-range is then used
  --fix-zero-ra  (or --fix-bad-RA-value-when-RA-should-be-zero): fix bad RA
values that result from a GBT bug, when the telescope position wavers above and
below RA = 0.0 during the observation (for details, do '--help --fix-zero-ra').
  --fix-zero-glon  (or --fix-bad-GLON-value-when-GLON-should-be-zero): similar
to '--fix-zero-ra' above, but for galactic longitude GLON
below RA = 0.0 during the observation.  If stored AZIMUTH and ELEVATIO values
agree with stored DEC but with RA = 0.0 [to within ( %.2f + <dSCAN> ) degrees,
where <dSCAN> can be set non-zero via '--dscan-cmp <dSCAN>' (or '-X <dSCAN>')],
then: if the stored RA value exceeds %.2f degrees [or 2.0 * ( %.2f + <dSCAN> ),
if <dSCAN> is negative], the stored RA value will be reset to zero.
NOTE that, by default, ALL the scans in the scan-input file will be copied to
the output scan-file, but RA values will be checked (and possibly set to zero)
ONLY for the scans selected by any specified range-options ('--index-range',
'--scan-range', '--stokes-range' [or '--scan-frac'], '--object-keep',
'--not-object', and/or '--object-range').  HOWEVER, if you specify '--extract'
as well as '--fix-zero-ra', then the usual range-option behavior will occur:
any scans not selected by the range-options will be ignored entirely (not copied to the output scan-file).
  --plot-file <plotFilename>  (or --plot-output-file ... or --plot ...
or -p ...): specify a text file for plots (do '--help --plot-file' for details).
  --def-plot-file  (or --default-plot-file): plot similarly, but obtain the

...): for PLOT OUTPUT ONLY, smooth spectra with a Hanning window of half-width

  --cmp-scan <compareScanFilename>  (or -x ... or --compare-input-scan-file ..):
Instead of computing new sidelobe scans, compare scans from the input scan-file
to those (having the same scan-number) in this other scan-file; a difference
summary is printed out at the end.  Unless you also specify '--list', an output
scan-file will be created, containing the differences of the spectra.  Note that
the options '--cmp-index' or '--cmp-pos' are better for SDFITS-files.
  --dscan-cmp <dSCAN>  (or -X ... or --scan-difference-for-comparison ...):
modifies the above, to compare scans at  scanNUM  in the input file with scans
at  scanNUM + <dSCAN>  in the compare-file  [or compare scans at  INDEX  with
scans at  INDEX + (int) <dSCAN>  , for '--cmp-index']; for the third comparison
case '--cmp-pos', the tolerance to say that two positions are equal is reset
 to ( %.1f + <dSCAN> ) arcsec.
  --cmp-index <compareScanFilename>  (or -y ... or --cmp-index-file ...
or --compare-input-scan-file-at-equal-index ...): the same as '--cmp-scan',
except that scans are compared to those having the same index in the other file
(i.e., the same row number, for SDFITS files).
  --cmp-pos <compareScanFilename>  (or -Y ... or --cmp ... or --cmp-pos-file ...
or --compare-input-scan-file-at-same-coordinates ...): the same, except that
scans are compared to those having the same RA,DEC (or l,b) coordinates in the
other file (to within %.1f arcsec).  This would usually be the case you want.
[Note that any of the above four options may be combined with the option
'--correct-stray', in order to specify a file containing strayscans (stray
radiation corrections) to be subtracted from the input spectra.  In this case,
the output file will contain stray-subtracted spectra, not simple differences.]
  --stat  (or --cmp-zero or --get-statistics-of-input-scan-file): get statistics
of the input file (by doing a comparison to spectra that are all zero).  This
option behaves as if '--list' were specified: no output file is allowed.
  --chi-sq <SIGMAbaseline>  (or --get-chi-squared-of-differences-not-rms ...
or --weighted-rms ...): in the comparison output, output a normalized version
of chi^2 (i.e., a weighted RMS) instead of the unweighted RMS, by weighting the
squares of the differences by 1/sigma^2 when computing the RMS (note that this
may not be meaningful when combined with '--stat').  The optional input value
of <SIGMAbaseline> is the uncertainty in the baseline correction; if negative
or omitted, the default of %.3f K will be used.
  --cmp-cal <FcmpCAL>  <STOKEScmp> <FcmpSTOKES>  (or --compare-file-calibration
... or --calibration-factor-for-compare-scan-file ...):
spectra by <FcmpCAL> as they are read in (or by <FcmpSTOKES>, if their Stokes
value matches <STOKESin>).  [Do '--help --cmp-cal' for details.]
the compare-file by the calibration factor <FcmpCAL> as they are read in [input
values of -1.0E+30 (or more negative) will be reset to the DEFAULT calibration
factor of unity].  If a character or integer STOKES value <STOKEScmp> is given,
spectra with that STOKES value will be multiplied by <FcmpSTOKES> instead.
While <FinCAL> (and <FcmpSTOKES>) will usually be close to unity, any value in
the range [ -1.0E+20 : 1.0E+20 ] will be accepted (in order to allow one to
produce an output file that is any linear combination of the input file and the
compare-file:  OUTspectrum = <FinCAL> * INspectrum - <FcmpCAL> * CMPspectrum ).
[If you specify '--correct-stray', you usually would NOT use '--cmp-cal'.]
  --cmp-unchanged  (or --do-not-correct-scans-to-vacuum-before-comparing): even
if one of the scans has been corrected for atmospheric absorption and the other
has not, do NOT correct them to be consistent (i.e., do NOT require that both
scans be corrected for atmospheric absorption before they are compared); just
compare the spectra as input (though spectra may be recalibrated, if '--in-cal'
or '--cmp-cal' was specified).  This is the DEFAULT case (unless you are really
calculating stray-corrected spectra, via '--correct-stray').
  --cmp-at-vac  (or --correct-scans-to-vacuum-before-comparing):  for SDFITS
files ONLY: ensure that both of the input scans are corrected for atmospheric
absorption before comparing them (this will also apply to the '--stat' option).
  --at-v-pixel  (or --compare-at-unshifted-velocity-pixel): compare spectra at
corresponding velocity pixels, ignoring the velocity frame (this is the DEFAULT
case, and so need not be specified).
  --at-v-frame  (or --compare-at-velocity-shifted-to-same-frame):  shift the
velocities in the compare-file to the same frame as those in the input file, and
compare at the same velocity by interpolating compare-file spectra in velocity.
[See also: options affecting the difference summary: '--v-exclude', '--v-bins',
'--set-v-bins', '--asym-v-bins', '--data-bins', and '--sys-unc-cmp';
and an option affecting a difference plotfile: '--cmp-plot-all'.]
  --v-convol-cmp <vFWHM>  (or --v-convol-stack ... or --v-convolve-compare-file
... or --convolve-compare-gaussian-velocity-FWHM-kms ...): for any compare-files
(see '--cmp') or stack-files (see '--stack-file'), convolve each of their input
spectra, as they are read in, with a gaussian of the specified FWMH in km/s
[zero yields the default of no convolution; negative or missing input yields
<vFWHM> = %.3f km/s (to convert a resolution of %.3f to %.3f km/s)].
[The following six options affect the difference-summary/statistics output that
is printed by the options '--cmp-scan', '--cmp-index', '--cmp-pos', or '--stat',
but they do NOT affect the output scan-file (if there is one).]
  --v-exclude <vLOexclude> <vHIexclude>  (or --v-exclude-range ...
or --exclude-LSR-velocity-region-from-comparison-rms ...): exclude the given
LSR velocity range from the sums computed for the difference-summary output (or
statistics output).  This option may be given multiple times, allowing one to
exclude an arbitrary number of velocity regions from the summary output.
[Note that any difference-output plot-files or scan-files are not affected.]
  --v-bins  (or --bin-comparisons-in-symmetric-velocity-regions): for comparison
summary output, bin the rms values, etc., in 4 ranges of the absolute magnitude
of the velocity (in the LSR frame), plus an overall comparison: |v| > %.0f km/s,
%.0f < |v| < %.0f, %.0f < |v| < %.0f, and |v| < %.0f km/s.  (This is the DEFAULT case.)
  --set-v-bins <vLObin> <vMIDbin> <vHIbin>  (or --set-abs-v-bins ...
or --set-LSR-velocity-bin-boundaries-for-comparisons ...): like the '--v-bins'
default case, except that the bin boundaries <vLObin> <vMIDbin> <vHIbin> are
input (negative or missing inputs yield respective defaults: %.0f, %.0f, %.0f km/s).
  --asym-v-bins <v1bin> <v2bin> <v3bin> <v4bin>  (or --set-asym-v-bins ...
or --set-asymmetric-LSR-velocity-bin-boundaries ...): use the velocity (rather
than its absolute magnitude) to bin comparison rms, etc.  If <v4bin> = 9999 or
more, then 4 bins are used (with bin-boundaries <v1bin> <v2bin> <v3bin>) plus
an overall (all-v) comparison; otherwise, 5 bins are used (with bin-boundaries
<v1bin> <v2bin> <v3bin> <v4bin>), and NO overall comparison.  An input value
of 9999 or more yields the respective default (%.0f, %.0f, %.0f, 9999 km/s).
  --data-bins  (or --bin-comparisons-in-data-size-regions): bin comparison rms
values, etc., in 4 data-magnitude regions: |Ta| < 0.01 K, 0.01 < |Ta| < 0.1,
0.1 < |Ta| < 1.0, and |Ta| > 1.0 K, where |Ta| refers to the specrum value with
the larger absolute magnitude (of the two being compared) at a given velocity.
  --sys-unc-cmp <UNCcmp>  (or --squelch-systematic-comparison-uncertainty ...):
for comparison summary-output purposes, any spectrum difference whose absolute
value is less than <UNCcmp> is reset to zero, and all other differences are
shifted closer to zero by <UNCcmp> (a negative input <UNCcmp> value is reset to
zero, i.e., no effect).  Note that this has no effect on any output scanfile.
  --cmp-plot-all  (or --compare-plot-all-scans): if plotting has been specified
[via '--plot-file' or '--default-plot-file'], then this option modifies the
default case, such that every compared scan gets a separate plot (the default
for comparison of two files is to plot only a single final summary).
  --no-in-sub  (or --no-systematic-subtract-from-input): use the spectra as
supplied from the input scan-file (without subtracting any systematic effects
from it).  This is the DEFAULT case, so it need not be specified.
  --set-in-sub <Vis> <Dis>  (or -m ... or --set-systematic-subtract-from-input
...): supply a velocity <Vis> in km/s and a systematic shift <Dis>
be inserted into a piecewise-linear systematic-shift-spectrum to be subtracted
from the input spectra (for details, do '--help --set-in-sub').
  --in-sub-file <fileInSub>  (or --file-for-systematic-subtract-from-input ...
or -M ...): specify a filename for a text file containing one or more lines
with pairs <Vis> <Dis> to be appended to the systematic-shift-spectrum
the velocity <Vis> is assumed to be in the same frame as the input spectrum,
unless you specify '--in-sub-LSR' (see below) or '--to-LSR'.  The <Vis>,<Dis>
value-pair will be appended to a piecewise-linear systematic-shift-spectrum,
which will be subtracted from each input spectrum when it is read in from the
input scan-file (AFTER any calibration factor has been applied: see '--in-cal').
[Note that ONLY spectra from the input file are affected, NOT spectra read in
from any files specified by the '--cmp' or '--stack-file' options.  Calculated
strayscans will not be affected, but there WILL be an effect on any output files
resulting from '--correct-stray', or from any versions of the '--extract',
'--stack', '--cmp', or '--plot' options.]  Any out-of-order velocities <Vis>
will be sorted to their correct place in the sequence; a velocity <Vis> that
differ by less than 0.001 km/s from a previously-supplied <Vis> replaces the
previous-supplied value.  A <Vis> value of -9999 or less means 'V at beginning
of input spectrum', and 9999 or more means 'V at end of input spectrum'.  This
option '--set-in-sub' can be repeated arbitrarily many times, or the following
to option '--set-in-sub' above).  Blank lines and lines starting with '#' will
be ignored; otherwise, the first two numbers on each line will be read in as
  --in-sub-same-frame  (or --systematic-subtract-from-input-is-in-same-frame):
assume that the systematic-shift-spectrum is in the same frame as the input
spectra from the input scan-file.  This option is the DEFAULT.  [Note that if
you specified '--to-LSR', the input spectra will be shifted into LSR, whatever
their frame in the input file, and thus <Vis> will be assumed to be in LSR too.]
  --in-sub-LSR  (or -K or --systematic-subtract-from-input-is-in-LSR-frame):
assume that the systematic-shift-spectrum is in the LSR frame (do any necessary
shift if the input spectrum from the input scan-file is in some other frame).
  --stack  (or --stack-stokes-pairs): for an SDFITS-file (e.g., from the GBT),
spectra in adjacent positions in the file that have the same position, date,
time, and velocity-range (but, presumably, different STOKES values) will be
added together, weighted by EXPOSURE values
 (for details, do '--help --stack').
  --stack-pos  (or --stack-spectra-at-same-sky-position): combine spectra from
adjacent indexes (rows) in the input file that have the same position on the
sky, to within %.1f arcsec (for details, do '--help --stack-pos').
  --stack-scan  (or --stack-spectra-at-same-scan-number): like '--stack-pos',
except that the adjacent spectra must have the same (integer part of the) scan
number (for details, do '--help --stack-scan').
  --stack-all  (or --stack-all-selected-spectra): like '--stack-scan', except
that ALL compatible spectra are combined
 (for details, do '--help --stack-all').
  --stack-n-pos <Nstack>  (or --stack-up-to-N-spectra-at-same-sky-position ...): 
  --stack-n-scan <Nstack>  (or --stack-up-to-N-spectra-at-same-scan-number ...): 
  --stack-n-all <Nstack>  (or --stack-up-to-N-selected-spectra ...):  These are
the same as the above options '--stack-pos', '--stack-scan', and '--stack-all',
respectively, except that at most <Nstack> consecutive spectra will be combined
into one output spectrum, before a new output spectrum is started.
  --stack-file <stackFile> <FstackCAL> <STOKESstack> <FstackSTOKES>
(or --stack-spectra-at-same-sky-position-from-file ...): for each spectrum from
the input file, stack it with spectra at the same sky-position from <stackFile>
(if you supply a calibration factor <FstackCAL>, it will override any supplied
via the '--in-cal' option).  [For details, do '--help --stack-file'.]
; the result will have the sum of
the EXPOSURE values and DURATION values (and the STOKES value will be set to 'I'
unless: either the option '--keep-stokes' was specified, or else the allowed
stokes-range contains only one STOKES value [see '--stokes-range']).
...For an SDD-file (from the 140-foot telescope), each scan whose scan number
has fractional part .01 will be stacked together with the corresponding scan
whose scan number has fractional part .02 (if such a scan exists).
[NOTE: stored quantities other than EXPOSURE and DURATION may not always be
handled correctly; this and the following '--stack' options are intended mainly
as a prelude to the creation of a cube, or as an aid to baseline checking or
comparisons of spectra (in that the stacked spectra have reduced random noise).]
sky [the same RA,DEC or GLON,GLAT to within ( %.1f + <dSCAN> ) arcsec; to set a
non-zero value of <dSCAN> (change the tolerance), use the '--dscan-cmp' option].
This is similar to '--stack', except that any number of consecutive spectra can
be combined, and that the velocity range is NOT checked (though the number of
velocity-axis points must be the same).  Spectra with different DATE-OBS values
will be combined only if the first DATE-OBS plus the sum of duration values at
that DATE-OBS is no more than <timeLimit> seconds earlier than the next DATE-OBS
(default <timeLimit> = %.1f seconds; this can be changed with '--time-limit'
option: note that specifying '--time-limit 1.0E+10' will allow jumps backwards
in time [which normally would start a new accumulation spectrum] as well as
forwards).  The combined spectrum has the same DATE-OBS, LST, etc. as the first
spectrum that was included (but has the summed EXPOSURE and total DURATION).
number.  The sky position (RA,DEC or GLON,GLAT) is averaged, if it differs
among the spectra combined.  The quantities VFRAME, AZIMUTH, ELEVATIO, RVSYS,
VELOSYS, RESTFREQ, OBSFREQ, FREQRES, and BANDWID are averaged, but their average
may not be quite correct, since the individual values averaged were not shifted
back to the first scan's DATE-OBS.  The quantities TAUZENIT, TAU, TAMBIENT,
PRESSURE, HUMIDITY, DEWPOINT, and TSYS are also averaged, which should yield
correct average values for these.
