 [except of course for those excluded
by any range-selection options that you may specify, e.g., '--index-range',
'--scan-range', '--stokes-range' (or '--scan-frac'), '--object-keep',
'--not-object', and/or '--object-range'].  Note that subdivision into several
output spectra can still occur if the difference between DATE-OBS values is big,
depending on <timeLimit> as described in '--stack-pos' above.
[optionally, you may also specify a calibration factor <FstackCAL> for spectra
from this file (and possibly an integer or character STOKES value <STOKESstack>
with an alternate factor <FstackSTOKES> to multiply any spectra with this STOKES
value); if you omit <FstackCAL>, the input-file calibration factor (if any) will
be used (see '--in-cal')].  It is expected that you will also specify one of the
'--make-GLS' options to create a cube [if not, this '--stack-file' option also
implies '--stack-pos --time-limit 1.0E+10', and the output SDFITS file will have
stacked spectra and summed EXPOSURE values, but the DURATION, position, and
date/time values will be determined solely from the input file].  Spectra in
different files will be stacked (if they are at the same position) regardless
of their DATE-OBS values; in addition, any range-selection options you specify
apply ONLY to the input file, NOT to the stack-file.  This '--stack-file' option
may be given up to 6 times, to stack several files with the input file.
  --get-GLS-grid  (or --get-gls-grid): for an input scan-file containing a GLS
grid [i.e., equally spaced in latitude and in longitude * cos(latitude)]: find
the grid-spacing values, the grid center and size (in Galactic coordinates, by
default), an alternate (possibly offset) center that is exactly on a grid-point,
and the maximum, rms, and mean variation from the 'exact' grid-point positions.
(Note that this option implies '--list': no output file is produced.)
  --make-GLS-cube <GLStol>  (or --make-gls-cube ... or --make-GLS ...): same as
above, except that a GLS-cube output FITS-file is created from the input SDFITS
file (note that this option implies '--to-LSR --stack').  Input spectra will be
copied to the grid-position closest to their position as specified in the input
SDFITS file (duplicates are overwritten and missing spectra are set to blank,
with a warning in each case [see also '--gls-fix']).  If velocity-endpoints are
the same for all scans to within the tolerance <GLStol> (in km/s), then the
input velocity/frequency axis will be used (note that a missing or non-positive
<GLStol> input value will yield the DEFAULT <GLStol> = %.3f km/s; a value larger
than 9999 km/s is an error). If spectrum endpoints differ by more than <GLStol>
in different scans, then the input spectra will be bi-quadratically interpolated
to a 'VELO-LSR' axis with a velocity-spacing close to that of the input spectra.
Note that, by default, axis-1 will be the velocity/frequency axis, axis-2 the
latitude (b or DEC), axis-3 the longitude (l or RA), and axis-4 the STOKES
value (this axis-order can be changed by the option '--gls-lbv', as described
below).  [See also '--lb-convol' and '--fine-to-car'.]  You MUST also specify
the output file name, via '--out <outFilename>' or '--out-cube <outFilename>'.
  --make-GLS-FELO <GLStol>  (or --make-gls-FELO ... or --make-GLS-cube-FELO
...): like '--make-GLS-cube', except that if input spectra are specified in
terms of 'FREQ' then the output will be converted to 'FELO-LSR' with the same
velocity-points (unless of course endpoints differ by more than <GLStol> km/s).
  --make-GLS-dv <dvGLS> <vGLS>  (or --make-gls-dv ... or --make-GLS-cube-dv
...): like '--make-gls-cube', except that the input velocity/frequency-axis is
always bi-quadratically interpolated to a 'VELO-LSR' axis with velocity-spacing
<dvGLS> in km/s (a missing or zero <dvGLS> value means 'use a velocity-spacing
close to that of the input spectra'; a magnitude larger than 9999.0 km/s or less
than 0.01 km/s is an error).  The reference pixel will be adjusted so that there
is a velocity grid-point exactly at <vGLS> km/s (a missing input value, or
<vGLS> below -99999.0 km/s, means that the maximum available velocity value is
used as this reference grid-point).
  --gls-radec  (or --GLS-grid-is-in-RA-DEC-coordinate-system or --GLS-RA-DEC):
the GLS grid to be obtained from the input SDFITS file should be in the RA,DEC
coordinate system (rather than the default of Galactic coordinates).
  --gls-lbv  (or --GLS-lbv or --GLS-RA-DEC-V): if a GLS cube will be output,
set axis-1 to longitude (rather than velocity), axis-2 to latitude, axis-3 to
velocity or frequency (rather than longitude), and axis-4 to STOKES.
  --gls-round  (or --round-GLS-grid-spacing-to-nearest-arcsec): after deriving
the GLS grid-spacing values from the grid-point positions in the input file,
round them to the nearest arcsecond (the DEFAULT is not to round them).
  --gls-spacing <db> <dl>  (or --set-GLS-grid-b-and-l-spacing-in-arcsec ...
or --set-GLS-grid-DEC-and-RA-spacing-in-arcsec ...): pre-set the expected GLS
grid-spacing in latitude and longitude (input values must be in arcseconds; a
negative or missing <dl> value will be set equal to <db>).  The DEFAULT is to
derive the grid-spacing from the point positions in the input file.
  --gls-center <bGLS> <lGLS>  (or --set-GLS-grid-center-b-and-l-in-degrees ...
or --set-GLS-grid-center-DEC-and-RA-in-degrees ...): after the GLS-grid has been
found, reset its center to these input b and l (or DEC and RA) values (in
degrees); an input value of -900 or less means 'use the calculated value'.
An error will result if these input values differ by more than a quarter of
a grid-spacing from the calculated central values.
  --gls-fix-point <indGLS> <bShift> <lShift>  (or --gls-fix ... or --GLS-fix ...
or --shift-GLS-point-when-finding-grid ...): for the input spectrum at the given
(integer) position-index <indGLS> in the input SDFITS file, shift the b and l
(or RA and DEC) values by the given amounts <bShift> and <lShift> respectively,
in arcsec, before trying to fit it into the GLS grid.  This option may be given
multiple times, to fix scans that are excessively far from their grid positions.
  --gls-pixel <ib> <il> <Kgls>  (or --gls-set-pixel ... or --GLS-pixel ...): when
a GLS-cube is being output, set the specified spectrum to the specified value.
The integers <ib> and <il> give the GLS-grid position (in ranges from 1 to N_b
and 1 to N_l), and the real value <Kgls> gives the value to which the (entire)
spectrum at that position should be set (<Kgls> must lie in the range -2100.0
to 2100.0, except that a value of -9999.0 or less means 'leave the spectrum
unchanged').  Only 6 decimal places will be kept (e.g., '4.E-7' would be rounded
to zero).  If either <ib> or <il> is zero, then <Kgls> will be used for all
spectra NOT specifically determined by other instances of this option.
  --lb-convol <lbFWHM> <Vtol>  (or --lb-convolve-fine-HI-cube ...
or --RA-DEC-convol ... or --gls-convol ... or --lb-convolve ...): if a GLS (or
Cartesian) cube will be output, first convolve it in the (l,b/RA,DEC)-plane
with a gaussian of FWHM = <lbFWHM> arcsec (zero means 'no convolution', and a
missing or negative input yields the DEFAULT of %.3f arcsec, which converts
a FWHM = 9.2 arcmin GBT beam to a 36-arcmin LD-survey beam).  If (other) fine-HI
cubes are being used to extend the edges of the convolution (see '--fine-HI'),
then <Vtol> gives the fractional V-grid mismatch allowed before V-interpolation
is required in the extension-cube (negative or missing value yields the DEFAULT
of %.2f).  [Note that a brute-force convolution is performed, which may take a
few minutes, but is an order of magnitude faster than computing sidelobes would
be.]  See also the '--make-gls' options.  If any (other) fine-HI input cubes are
specified (see '--fine-HI'), then these cubes are used (where available) beyond
the edges of this cube that is being convolved (the two edge-control options
below apply only where no fine-HI cubes can be used to extend the edges).
  --convol-mirror  (or --cube-edges-act-as-mirrors-in-convolution
or --convol-mirror-edge): if the above '--lb-convol' option is specified, treat
the l,b-edges of the cube as mirrors for convolution purposes (the default is
to treat anything beyond the cube edge as if it were zero [yielding convolved
values that drop by a factor of order 2 to 4 as one approaches the cube edges]).
This option thus allows one to have reasonable (but NOT necessarily correct)
convolved values closer to the cube edges than in the default case.
  --convol-ignore  (or --give-no-weight-to-blank-spectra-in-convolution
or --convol-ignore-blank): if the above '--lb-convol' option is specified, give
no weight in the convolution to spectra beyond the l,b-edges of the cube (nor to
blank spectra).  [Typically, this gives results similar to '--convol-mirror'
above: a bit faster, but it may sometimes not be as good.]
  --refract  (or --include-atmospheric-refraction): include refraction; this
depends on local temperature, pressure, and humidity, and can have a significant
effect on the sidelobes (for details, do '--help --refract'; see also '--amb').
This option is the DEFAULT case (and thus need not be specified).
affects the position in the sidelobes of the galactic positions, and increases
the amount of the sky that is above the horizon (most refraction takes place
above the elevations of the ridges that define the horizon, so the optical
horizon is assumed to be the same as the observed radio horizon position: even
if given the distance or elevation of each horizon point, little improvement
could be obtained from relaxing this assumption).  NOTE that the RA,DEC (or
galactic l,b) values obtained from the stored scan are assumed to have been
corrected for refraction, and the stored scan azimuth and elevation values are
ignored.  If available, the atmospheric pressure, temperature, and relative
humidity (or dew point) from the scan are used for the refraction calculation;
if unavailable, then values are interpolated in time from an ambient-conditions
input file, if one was specified (see '--amb'); otherwise, 'typical' values are
used (default P = %.1f mmHg, T = %.1f degrees Celsius, and RH = %.2f).  This
 option is the DEFAULT case (therefore it can be omitted).
  --refract-obs-beam  (or --include-refraction-but-beam-was-uncorrected):
include refraction, but assume that the telescope beam position from the scan
file was NOT corrected earlier for refraction, i.e., that the beam position
defines the observed elevation (this is not usually a useful option).
  --refract-approx  (or --include-horizon-refraction-ignore-sidelobe-shift):
include the effect of refraction on the position of the horizon and on the
extinction calculation, but do NOT include the shift in the position of the
sidelobe pattern.  This can speed up the 140-foot calculation (but not the GBT)
by about 30 percent; it can yield errors of several percent (up to half as
large as ignoring refraction altogether).
  --no-refract  (or --ignore-atmospheric-refraction): ignore atmospheric
refraction entirely; this can lead to errors of up to 10 percent.
  --refract-tlr <troposphereTemperatureLapseRate> <epsConvergeRefract>
(or -refraction-reset-temperature-lapse-rate-and-eps ...): reset the value of
<troposphereTemperatureLapseRate>, the troposphere temperature lapse rate in
degrees K per metre used for refraction calculations (a negative input value
yields the DEFAULT of %.6f), and <epsConvergeRefract>, the refraction
convergence limit (missing or negative input yields the DEFAULT of %.1e;
only values in the range [%.1e:%.1e] are allowed).
  --use-RESTFREQ  (or --use-RESTFREQ-for-frequency-at-zero-velocity
or --use-RESTFRQ or --use-restfreq): For SDFITS files that contain RESTFREQ
(sometimes spelled RESTFRQ), use the input value of this keyword or column to
give the frequency corresponding to zero velocity when converting frequencies
to velocities (the default is to use the HI-line frequency of %.1f Hz).
  --def-tau  (or --use-default-tau-zenith): always use the default tau_zenith
value (see option '--set-def-tau' below) when computing the optical depth of
the atmosphere (ignore any TAUZENIT or TAU values from input files).  This is
the DEFAULT case, so this option need not be given explicitly.
  --use-tau  (or --use-TAUZENIT-or-TAU-from-scan-file):  if TAUZENIT is present
in the input scan-file (or in an ambient-conditions file), its value is used
instead of the default tau_zenith value (see option '--set-def-tau' below).
Otherwise, if TAU is available from an input file, use it to rescale the value
  --set-def-tau <tauZenith>  (or --set-default-tau-zenith-value ...): reset the
tau_zenith value to <tauZenith> (negative input yields DEFAULT of %.6f);
this is the value that will be used if '--use-tau' (see above) has not been
specified, or if TAUZENIT and TAU are unavailable.
  --fix-tau-gbtidl  (or --fix-GBTIDL-atmospheric-correction or --fix-gbtidl):
if the GBTIDL atmospheric correction factor  exp{ 0.0084 / sin(el) } / 0.99
had been applied to the spectrum (or * 0.99, prior to Jan 8, 2007), then remove
this factor and replace it with the correct atmospheric correction factor.
  --warn-tau-gbtidl  (or --warn-of-GBTIDL-atmospheric-correction): If the GBTIDL
atmospheric correction facter was used, output a warning message (default case).
  --deny-tau-gbtidl  (or --deny-GBTIDL-atmospheric-correction or --deny-gbtidl):
If the GBTIDL atmospheric correction facter was used, stop with error message.
  --OBS-freq-const  (or --OBS-frequency-is-constant-LSR-frequency-drifts
or --LSR-freq-drifts): Assume that the frequency observed at the telescope is
held constant during the integration, so the frequency in the LSR frame drifts
during this time (this is the DEFAULT case, and need not be specified).
  --OBS-tracks-LSR  (or --OBS-frequency-drifts-LSR-frequency-is-constant
or --LSR-freq-const or --OBS-freq-drifts): Assume that the frequency observed
at the telescope is caused to drift during the integration time in such a way
that the frequency in the LSR frame is held constant.
[Note that neither of the above two options is applied with absolutely complete
consistency, but any errors from this should be small.]
  --calc-v-lsr  (or --calculate-v-telescope-to-LSR): calculate the velocity
difference between the scan velocity-coordinate frame and the Local Standard of
Rest (LSR) frame, using the DATE-OBS and the LST (rather than using the value of
VFRAME from the input scan).  This is the DEFAULT case (option can be omitted).
  --use-VFRAME  (or --use-vframe or --use-VFRAME-for-v-telescope-to-LSR): For
SDFITS files that contain VFRAME: scans which have VELDEF = 'RADI-LSR', but have
spectra defined in the telescope ('OBS') frame (i.e., with CTYPEn = 'VELO-OBS',
'FELO-OBS', or 'FREQ-OBS', where n is the velocity/frequency-axis), will use
the value of VFRAME (from the input scan) instead of actually calculating the
velocity-difference between the telescope frame and the LSR frame (i.e., use the
value that was calculated/stored by the software that produced the SDFITS file).
This option behaves as if '--check-VFRAME 0' was also specified (see below).
[Note that velocity differences are applied non-relativistically in general,
although conversion of frequencies to velocities uses relativistic formulae.]
  --check-VFRAME <NwarnVFRAME> <dVFRAME>  (or --check-vframe ...
or --check-VFRAME-with-v-telescope-to-LSR ...): For SDFITS files that contain
VFRAME (and scans having VELDEF='RADI-LSR' but spectrum velocities in the 'OBS'
frame): compare VFRAME with the corresponding calculated velocity difference,
and output a warning message for the first <NwarnVFRAME> cases that are worse
than <dVFRAME> km/s (a negative input <NwarnVFRAME> means 'output a warning
message for any mismatch worse than <dVFRAME>'; a negative or missing <dVFRAME>
value yields the DEFAULT of %.3f km/s).  Even if <NwarnVFRAME> = 0, a one-line
summary will be output when the sidelobe program exits, giving the number of
scans checked (which may differ from the number of scans read in), the worst
mismatch, and the number of cases worse than <dVFRAME> (if any).
  --calc-v-tel  (or --calculate-telescope-velocity): calculate the velocity
difference between the scan velocity-coordinate frame and the telescope frame
(rather than using the value of RVSYS from the input scan).  This is the DEFAULT
case (this option can be omitted).
  --use-rvsys  (or --use-rvsys-for-telescope-velocity): Use the value of RVSYS
from the input scan for the velocity-shift between the scan velocity-coordinate
frame (e.g., LSR) and the telescope frame (rather than calculating this).
[Note that this option is kept for compatibility with earlier program versions;
it may yield wildly incorrect results in some circumstances.]
  --check-rvsys <rvchkOutLevel> <dVchk>
(or --check-rvsys-with-calculated-telescope-velocity ...): compare the value of
RVSYS from the input scan with the calculated velocity difference between the
telescope frame and that of the stored spectrum; if the two do not agree with
each other within the tolerance <dVchk> (default %.3f km/s), output an error
message, depending on the value of <rvchkOutLevel>, and the value of <logLevel>
specified (e.g., via '--log').  If <rvchkOutLevel> is negative or not specified,
and you have also specified '--quiet', then only a final summary is output, with
the number of significant disagreements (and the largest one).  Otherwise, for
any scan with a significant disagreement (larger than <dVchk>), the larger of
<rvchkOutLevel> and <logLevel> will control the amount of output for that scan.
  --calc-lst  (or --calculate-LST-from-UT): Calculate the Local Sidereal Time
(LST) from the telescope longitude and the DATE-OBS (and possibly TIME) value
from the input scan (Universal Time and Date); ignore LST from the input scan.
This is the DEFAULT case, so this option can be omitted unless you want to
override a previously-supplied option.
  --use-lst  (or --use-LST-from-scan): use the value of LST (the Local Sidereal
Time) from the input scan, if it is available (if not, LST is calculated).
Stored LST occasionally have 60-sec errors (do '--help --calc-lst' for details).
[NOTE that the LST value stored in GBT files may sometimes (but not always)
differ from that calculated using DATE-OBS and the telescope longitude.  The
difference usually is only a few seconds, but a difference of 62.4 seconds has
been seen for a case which had a value of DATE-OBS = '2006-01-15T08:26:60.00'
(by considering neighboring values, this DATE-OBS was seen to be correct, though
it should of course have been expressed as '2006-01-15T08:27:00.00' instead,
but apparently it had been incorrectly interpreted by GBT software, yielding an
additional error in the stored LST value of 60 seconds on top of the difference
of 2.4 seconds that would otherwise have resulted, for a total error equivalent
to a quarter of a degree --- note that the sidelobe program interprets this
admittedly peculiar DATE-OBS value correctly).]
  --check-az-el <tolAZEL>  (or --check-azimuth-and-elevation-vs-sky-position
...): for each input scan, check that the telescope azimuth and elevation from
the input SDD or SDFITS file agrees within <tolAZEL> degrees with the values
calculated from the source position (via the date/time and telescope position);
if <tolAZEL> is omitted or negative, the default tolerance is %.3f degrees.
  --time-limit <timeLimit> <timeVlimit> <NsumLimit> <sideWtLimit>
(or --maximum-scan-integration-time-segment ...): A scan with an extremely long
observation period (DURATION or EXPOSURE) will be divided into time-segments
according to these values (alternatively, if a '--stack' option is given, then
<timeLimit> is a tolerance to tell whether scans are adjacent in time).  The
default is <timeLimit> = %.1f sec (do '--help --time-limit' for details).
no more than <timeVlimit> seconds (a missing or negative input value yields the
DEFAULT of %.1f sec; <timeVlimit> = 0.0 means 'use just one such segment').
Velocity offsets are computed (and spectra are summed) at the middle of each of
these time-segments.  Each such segment is subdivided into sub-segments of no
more than <timeLimit> seconds (negative input yields the DEFAULT %.1f sec,
corresponding to a sky rotation of %.3f degrees; <timeLimit> = 0.0 means 'use
just one such segment').  For each spectrum, the ( sidelobe * atmospheric
attenuation * solid angle ) value is evaluated at the middle of each time
sub-segment, and the average of these is used as the weight for that spectrum
(at that galactic latitude and longitude).  To reduce round-off error, the
total spectrum sums are accumulated in chunks of <NsumLimit> spectra (missing
or negative input yields the DEFAULT of %d; <NsumLimit> = 0 means 'use the
best estimate') [this should have negligible effect unless the observation
period is very long and/or <timeVlimit> is very short].  Spectra are summed
only if their weight exceeds <sideWtLimit> (missing or negative input yields
the DEFAULT of %.1e); note that this limit should be much smaller than the
minimum solid angle used, which is 3.0E-6 (unless the option '--domega-full' or
'--domega-orig' was specified, in which case the minimum solid angle is 6.6E-9).
[Note: there is a different meaning if a '--stack' option is given; in this
case, <timeLimit> is a tolerance to tell whether scans are adjacent in time.]
  --use-start-time  (or --use-time-from-start-of-scan): the velocity offset and
the sidelobe contributions will always be evaluated just once, at the time
corresponding to the beginning (not middle) of the period when the scan was
obtained (this option resets <timeLimit> and <timeVlimit> to so indicate).
This yields errors for integration times of a minute or more: NOT RECOMMENDED.
  --period-mul <periodMul>  (or -P ... or --multiplicative-factor-for-duration
...): provide a factor to multiply DURATION, to get the true full duration per
observation point (this is often unity, occasionally 2.0).  For SDFITS files, by
default the first few spectra are checked to try to get a value automatically.
(For details, do '--help --period-mul'.)
...): if the stored DURATION (or EXPOSURE) value in the input scan-file does
not reflect the actual time-period during which the observations were taken,
then, for the purpose of determining sidelobe-pattern azimuth and elevation
values, multiply the stored duration value by <periodMul>.  DEFAULT: if not
supplied by the user, then, for an SDFITS file ONLY, the sidelobe program will
attempt to determine a reasonable value by looking at the DATE-OBS and DURATION
values of the first few spectra in the file [for example, if the first two
spectra have different Stokes parameters but both have the same sky position and
DATE-OBS value (date/time), while the third spectram has a different DATE-OBS,
then: for the first two spectra, if the SUM of the DURATION values is roughly
equal to the difference in DATE-OBS between the first and third spectra,
<periodMul> will be set to 2.0, while if the first two spectra BOTH have
DURATION values roughly equal to the difference in DATE-OBS between the first
and third spectra, <periodMul> will be set to 1.0].  (For SDD-files from the
140-foot telescope, the default <periodMul> is unity.)  Note that, for short
durations (less than about a minute), the effect of such a finite duration is
negligibly small.                                       ...SPECIAL CASES:
If <periodMul> is negative, then the date and time stored in the input file are
assumed to refer to the end of the observation, rather than the beginning.
If <periodMul> is between 100.0 and 10000.0, then <periodMul> / 1000.0 will be
used, and the date and time stored in the input file are assumed to refer to
the middle of the observation.  [Note that these special cases should not be
needed for files conforming to the SDFITS standard.]
  --dtx4  (or --multiplicative-factor-of-four-for-duration): set <periodMul> = 4
[this may be appropriate for raw GBT SDFITS-files, if they consist of sets of 4
spectra with the same DATE-OBS value, and (as sometimes happens) DURATION values
of 1/4 of the interval to the next DATE-OBS value (but you should
not be computing stray radiation for raw GBT SDFITS-files anyway)].
  --dtx2  (or --multiplicative-factor-of-two-for-duration): set <periodMul> = 2
[this may be appropriate for baseline-corrected GBT SDFITS-files, if they have
sets of 2 spectra with different polarizations but the same DATE-OBS value, and
(as sometimes happens) DURATION values equal to half the interval to the next
  --dtx1  (or --multiplicative-factor-of-unity-for-duration):  <periodMul> = 1
[this may be appropriate for GBT SDFITS-files if (as sometimes happens) the
DURATION value for each spectrum is equal to the interval to the next DATE-OBS
value (rather than the sum of DURATION values being equal to this interval)].
  --cube-vchunk <Nchunk>  (or --HI-survey-cube-file-lbv-chunk ...): for a
(non-default or original) HI-survey FITS cube-file where axis-1 is not velocity,
define how it will be input in chunks (for details, do '--help --cube-vchunk').
(non-default or original) HI-survey FITS cube-file containing an image where
the velocity-axis FOLLOWS the longitude- and latitude-axes, read the cube in
chunks of <Nchunk> velocity pixels, where each chunk contains ALL longitudes
and latitudes (a negative input value yields the DEFAULT of <Nchunk> = %d).
[This uses <Nchunk> * 0.25 Mb of extra memory storage while reading; however,
an original FITS file may take hours to read if you set <Nchunk> = 0 (meaning
'read in one spectrum at a time').  Even <Nchunk> = 1 is much faster.]
  --fix-cube-10  (or --fix-HI-survey-cube-below-minus-10-K): for the LD/IAR/UB
'version 2' HI data of 2004, which has a cut-off at -10 K, fix the spectra to
include the excursions below -10 K (down to -118.1 K), if possible (for more
details, do '--help --fix-cube-10').  This is the default case.
include the excursions below -10 K (down to -118.1 K), if possible.  [For these
very-negative spectrum values, this fix-up is taken from the old LD/Parkes/Bell/
South 'version 1' data, and is stored with a resolution of 0.1425 K rather than
0.0025 K.]  Note that this option generally has no effect when reading in a
modified cube-file created by this sidelobe program.  This option is the
default, and need not be specified.
  --no-fix-cube-10  (or --do-not-fix-HI-survey-cube-below-minus-10-K): for the
LD/IAR/UB 'version 2' HI data of 2004, do NOT fix the spectra below -10 K.
  --fix-spurious  (or --fix-HI-survey-cube-spurious-features): for the LD-survey
HI cube, fix known spurious peaks/dips (you can do '--help --fix-spurious' for
the details).  This is the default case.
HI cube, fix any known spurious peaks or dips (due to interference) in the
spectra by linear interpolation in velocity.  [Note that this can only be done
when reading in from an IMAGE cube-file such as 'Cent-big.fits' --- 39 of the
spurious features to fix are from the partial-galactic-plane survey of Higgs et
al. (2000) AJ, 120, 2471 and (2005) AJ, 129, 2750; the other 59 features were
found via comparisons to convolved versions of GBT observations far from the
Galactic plane.]  This option is the DEFAULT case.
  --no-fix-spurious  (or --do-not-fix-HI-survey-cube-spurious-features): do not
fix these known spurious features in the input spectra.
  --fix-cal  (or --fix-HI-survey-cube-calibration or --fix-calibration): for the
original (non-default) LD-survey HI cube, fix known mis-calibrated spectra (you
can do '--help --fix-cal' for the details).  This is the default case.
  --no-fix-cal  (or --do-not-fix-HI-survey-cube-calibration): do not use stored
re-calibration factors to fix input spectra from a FITS-image HI cube.
  --cal-tol <calApplyTol>  (or --tolerance-for-HI-survey-cube-calibration ...):
Mis-calibration tolerance for '--fix-cal' (for details, do '--help --cal-tol').
LD-survey HI cube, fix spectra known to be mis-calibrated by more than a given
tolerance (default %.3f; see '--cal-tol'), using the program-internal stored
re-calibration factors that were originally obtained using '--cal-cube' (see
below).  [Note that the only sky regions that can be corrected are those where
other HI observations are available to provide calibration spectra.]   This is
the DEFAULT case when you are reading an original (non-default) FITS-3D-image
HI cube (such as 'Cent-big.fits').
A re-calibration factor is applied to an HI-survey only if the factor is larger
than  1 + <calApplyTol>  or less than  1 / ( 1 + <calApplyTol> )  ; negative
input values yield the DEFAULT of %.3f (this re-calibration tolerance applies
both to '--fix-cal' above and '--cal-cube' below).
  --cal-cube <calibrationCubeFile> <flagFavg>  (or --calibration-cube ...
or --cube-used-to-check-HI-survey-calibration ...):
check the LD-survey calibration (for details, do '--help --cal-cube').
multiple times, to specify HI-cube-files that will be used to re-calibrate the
HI-survey.  This is done after any fixes from '--fix-spurious' or '--fix-cal',
and is possible only if none of the input spectra are combined (i.e., only if
one specifies '--domega-full' or '--domega-orig').  For each calibration-cube,
re-calibration factors will be obtained for each HI-survey spectrum in that
cube's area by a weighted average for spectrum values over 1 K (weighted by the
square of the spectrum value) [or by the ratio of the peak-integrals, if one
specifies '--integral-fcal' (below)].  If the optional integer flag <flagFavg> is
missing or zero, then these re-calibration factors will be shifted so that their
unweighted mean is unity (from the assumption that the LD-survey is correctly
calibrated on average, with only a few exceptions; specifying '--wt-avg-fcal'
or '--integral-fcal' gives a weighted mean).  If <flagFavg> = -1, then the shift
shift from the previous cube is used (this is useful for cubes with very few
spectra).  If <flagFavg> = 1, then no such shift will be done (implying that
you believe your input calibration-cube already has the same mean calibration
as the LD-survey cube).  These (shifted) re-calibration factors will then be
applied to the LD-survey spectra, and values printed to the terminal (in C-code
format suitable to allow their addition to the 'readhisurvey.c' program file).
  --wt-avg-fcal  (or --weighted-average-cube-calibration-normalization
or --wt-norm-fcal): weight calibration factors of strong spectra more than those
of weak spectra when computing the mean calibration factor for normalization
of input calibration-cubes (specifed via '--cal-cube' above), i.e., computing
the above calibration-factor-shift (default is to weight all of the calibration
factors equally when taking the average over the calibration-cube).
  --integral-fcal  (or --use-peak-integral-for-HI-survey-recalibration
or --cal-int): use the 'peak-integral' (rather than the weighted peak-height)
to obtain HI-survey spectrum re-calibration factors from input calibration-cubes
(specifed via '--cal-cube' above).  For any spectrum-comparison, the velocity
points included in the integral comprise those where EITHER spectrum value is
part of its 'peak' (and neither spectrum value is null).  The 'peak' is defined
as all points above 1 K, plus all points above 0.5 K that are also above half
the maximum spectrum value.  The 'peak-integral' is also used as the weight for
the spectrum's calibration factor, when obtaining the mean.
  --plot-cal <calibrationPlotFilename>  (or --plot-HI-survey-calibration-cubes
...): For all calibration-cubes specified by '--cal-cube' above, plot the parts
of all spectra used to get the calibration factors to this text plot-file.
  --min-plot-cal <dFClo> <dFChi>  : only plot extreme calibration cases, when
the option '--plot-cal' is specified (for details, do '--help --min-plot-cal').
  --min-plot-cal <dFClo> <dFChi>
(or --minimum-dF-to-plot-HI-survey-calibration-cubes ...): the calibration-cube
spectrum will be plotted (for the '--plot-cal' case) ONLY if the (unshifted)
fractional re-calibration factor is NOT inside the range from <dFClo> to <dFChi>
(the DEFAULT is 0.0 0.0 [i.e., plot all spectra]; if the second input value
<dFChi> is missing, this yields a symmetric range from -|<dFClo>| to |<dFClo>|).
  --hist-cal <calibrationHistogramFilename>  (or --cal-hist-file ...
or --HI-survey-calibration-cube-histogram-filename ...): specify a text output
file, to which is written all the calibration factors (with their weights) from
the new cubes specified via '--cal-cube' above.
  --out-cube <outCubeFilename>  (or -C ... or --output-survey-HI-cube-filename
...): supply a name for a MODIFIED output HI-cube FITS file to be created,
containing the LD-survey data as modified by the other input arguments; the
DEFAULT is NOT to create any such file.
 --only-create-cube  (or --only-read-cube or --only-create-HI-survey-cube):
stop after reading the HI-survey cube-file (and possibly creating a MODIFIED
version and/or plotting it); no input scan-file need be specified.  (this
option is useful only with options such as '--out-cube', '--plot-cube',
'--cal-cube', '--plot-cal', and/or '--hist-cal').
  --plot-cube  (or --plot-HI-survey-cube): produce at least 361 plotfiles, one
for each galactic latitude from the stored HI survey spectra; each plotfile
will contain the spectra for all longitudes at that latitude.
  --only-plot-cube  (or --only-plot-HI-survey-cube): this is the same as
specifying both '--only-read-cube' and '--plot-cube'.
  --domega-def  (or --domega-larger-tilings-allowed): combine the LD-survey
longitude-points so as to get a basic ('level 0') tiling with nearly-uniform
solid angles (about 0.5x0.5 degrees square) per (l,b) point, and also add (l,b)
tilings at levels 1, 2, and 3 (1x1, 2x2, and 4x4 degrees square, possibly with
different spectral resolution: see option '--v-comb'); these latter tile levels
are used if the sidelobe is flat enough.  This is the DEFAULT case: this option
can be omitted unless you want to override a previously-supplied option.
  --domega-uniform  (or --domega-as-close-as-possible-to-uniform): combine some
of the LD-survey longitude-points so as to get a nearly-uniform solid angle per
stored (l,b) point (about 0.5x0.5 degrees square); this is not significantly
better than the default '--domega-def' (and runs much slower).
  --domega-full  (or --domega-corrected): do not combine any LD-survey points
(solid angle becomes small near galactic poles), but DO use the correct formula
for the solid angle (results are similar to the '--domega-uniform' case).
  --domega-orig  (or --domega-original-error): do not combine any LD-survey
points (as for '--domega-full'), but use the original (INCORRECT) formula for
the solid angle (which vanishes at galactic polar caps, thus ignoring their
contribution, and is less accurate elsewhere).  NOT RECOMMENDED.
  --cut <Bvcut> <Nedge> <Nsmooth> <PowerNcomb>  (or --b-v-cut ...
or --survey-spectrum-cutoff-level ...):
 ignore ("trim") the ends of spectra in
the LD-survey HI-cube wherever their running average is less than <Bvcut> (note
that by default this is not done; for details, do '--help --cut').
 'END-TRIMMING' parameters: note that
<Bvcut> must be given, but the others will be given default values if omitted
(without this option, or for input values of -1, the defaults can be overridden
by a modified HI-survey cube-file created by this program).  Only spectra from
3-D image cube-files can be trimmed (not files containing spectra that were
already trimmed or already combined).  If <Bvcut> is positive, then the ends of
the HI cube survey-spectra may be trimmed outside the outermost pixel where a
<Nsmooth>-point running average of the spectrum exceeds <Bvcut> K (where DEFAULT
<Nsmooth> = %d, must be an odd integer, -9 will be reset to default; a zero
<Bvcut> value means 'do NOT do this end-trimming', while <Bvcut> = -9.0 will be
reset to the DEFAULT value of %.3f, and a value of -5.0 will be reset to the
'reasonable' but non-default value <Bvcut> = %.3f).  Outside this trim-point,
a padding of <Nedge> velocity-pixels will be kept (a value of -9 will yield the
DEFAULT of %d).  For cases where Ncomb survey-spectra are combined into a
single spectrum, the value of <Bvcut> will temporarily be reduced by a factor
of Ncomb to the power <PowerNcomb> (the maximum allowed value is 0.5; a value
of -9.0 will be reset to the DEFAULT of %.3f).  Note that <Bvcut> can affect
only velocities between <Vmin> and <VloKeep>, or between <Vmax> and <VhiKeep>
(see the option '--v-lim' below).
  --end-trim-avg  (or --use-average-in-end-trimmed-spectrum-regions): if the
'end-trimming' is not turned off (see the above option '--cut'), then when
the sidelobes are being accumulated, in a trimmed region the average spectrum
value over the trimmed region will be used (possibly with some slope factor).
This is the DEFAULT case, so this option can be omitted.
  --no-trim-avg  (or --set-end-trimmed-spectrum-regions-to-zero): assume that
the spectrum is zero in any trimmed region (see the above option '--cut').
  --trim-slope <avgSlope>  (or --fractional-slope-for-end-trim-average ...):
when adding the average over end-trimmed spectrum regions (see the above option
'--end-trim-avg'), do not add a constant, but a value that decreases linearly
(with a fractional slope <avgSlope>) from the trimmed end-point out to where
the end of the spectrum was originally, starting from (1.0+<avgSlope>) times
the average value and decreasing to (1.0-<avgSlope>) times the average value;
the maximum allowed value is <avgSlope> = 1.0 (DEFAULT is %.3f).
  --v-lim <Vmin> <Vmax> <VloKeep> <VhiKeep>
(or --survey-spectrum-velocity-limit ...): the HI-survey-cube spectra will be
truncated if necessary so that their range lies inside [<Vmin>:<Vmax>] km/s
(DEFAULT is [%.1f:%.1f]; a positive <Vmin>, or a negative or missing <Vmax>,
will be reset to the default).  If '--cut' (see above) is specified, ends not
be trimmed inside [<VloKeep>:<VhiKeep>] km/s (for details, do '--help --v-lim').
will be reset to the default).  In addition, if 'end-trimming' is not turned
off (see the option '--cut' above), then trimming of the negative end of the
spectrum will not extend above <VloKeep>, nor will trimming of the positive end
extend below <VhiKeep> (note that these two parameters MAY have some effect
unless <VloKeep> is positive and larger than <Vmax>, and <VhiKeep> is negative
and less than <Vmin>; missing values yield the DEFAULT of <VloKeep> = 9999.0,
<VhiKeep> = -9999.0, i.e., 'no effect').  If this option is absent, then the
defaults may be overridden by the HI survey cube-file (unless you have instead
specified '--def', '--fast', or '--acc').
  --levels <levelMin> <levelMax> <levelAtHorizon>  (or --tiling-levels
or --allowed-tile-size-level-range ...): for the default ('--domega-def')
HI-survey-variable-tiling case: never descend below level <levelMin> (negative
value yields DEFAULT of 0, i.e., 0.5x0.5 degrees), but always descend as far as
<levelMax> (negative or missing value yields DEFAULT of 3, i.e., 4x4 degrees);
near the horizon, always descend at least to <levelAtHorizon> (negative or
missing value yields DEFAULT of %d).  Within the allowed range, the choice of
level to use at a given position depends on the variation of the sidelobe over
the given tile, and on any values specified via the option '--deltas'.
  --deltas <D0> <D1> <D2> <D3>  (or --allowed-tile-variation ...): for the
default ('--domega-def') HI-survey-variable-tiling case: descend below level n
when the sidelobe variation over tile-size(level_n) reaches or exceeds <Dn>
(missing or negative values yield DEFAULT of %.6f).  You must specify <D0>,
although at present only <D1>, <D2>, and <D3> have any effect.
  --v-comb <nv0> <nv1> <nv2> <nv3>  (or --combine-HI-cube-velocities ...):
specify how many adjacent velocity pixels should be combined from the input
HI-survey cube at each tiling level (for details, do '--help --v-comb').
HI-survey cube to produce (possibly reduced-resolution) spectra used to get
stray corrections.  Input value(s) of -1 mean 'use the DEFAULT values of
{<nv0...3>} = {%d,%d,%d,%d} unless overridden by the HI survey cube-file', while
input value(s) of -9 mean 'require that the default value be used'; note that a
value of 1 means no reduction of the 1-km/s velocity resolution.  The value of
<nv0> is used for the survey's basic 0.5x0.5 degree tiling of the galactic
plane; the other values are used respectively for the level 1, 2, and 3 tilings
(1x1, 2x2, and 4x4 degrees), and thus have no effect if you specify any of the
options '--domega-uniform', '--domega-full', or '--domega-original'.
  --smooth-horizon <fracR_atHorizon>  (or --frac-smooth-horizon ...
or --fraction-of-tile-smoothed-over-horizon ...): for an LD-survey (l,b)-tile
turn its weight off smoothly as the central <fracR_atHorizon> (default %.2f) of
its diameter crosses the horizon (for details, do '--help --smooth-horizon').
position with solid angle dOmega and corresponding effective radius R_domega:
the solid angle is reduced from its nominal value when the tile's center is
less than a distance ( <fracR_atHorizon> * R_domega ) above the horizon; it is
reduced to zero when the tile's center is this distance below the horizon.
This 'smooths' the horizon (DEFAULT is <fracR_atHorizon> = %.2f).  The position
at which the sidelobe is evaluated is also shifted to a corresponding point
above the horizon (for the 140-foot, if refraction is ignored then this shift
is also ignored [see option '--no-refract']).  A value <fracR_atHorizon> = 0.0
means 'do not do this smoothing: ignore positions centered below the horizon,
and use full weight for positions centered above the horizon'.
  --flat-horizon <flatHorizonLevel>  (or --flat-horizon-level ...): [TEST CASE]:
instead of using the measured horizon for the relevant telescope, use a flat
horizon at an elevation of <flatHorizonLevel> degrees (must not exceed 89 deg,
but can be negative, as low as -99 deg [which would mean all 4*PI was visible,
i.e., Earth was transparent]; a value of -100 deg or lower is reset to 5.0 deg).
  --v-lin  (or -l or --velocity-linear-interpolation): interpolate spectra
linearly in velocity (from the LD-survey HI cube used to obtain the sidelobe
corrections).  This is the DEFAULT case, so this option can be omitted.
  --v-quad  (or -v or --v-biquad or --velocity-biquadratic-interpolation): make
use of two overlapping quadratics for the velocity interpolation: note that this
bi-quadratic interpolation is similar to a spline, but much faster (it takes
only about twice as long as linear interpolation).
  --v-spline  (or -V or --velocity-spline-interpolation): use cubic spline
velocity interpolation (this was used by the original version of the sidelobe
program; it takes ten times as long as linear interpolation).  NOT RECOMMENDED.
  --v-near  (or -N or --velocity-nearest-point-interpolation): just use the
nearest velocity pixel (this is only a few percent faster than using linear
interpolation, and somewhat less accurate).  NOT RECOMMENDED.
  --calc-side <varyVersion>  (or --calculate-sidelobe-matrix ...): compute a
pre-calculated sidelobe matrix and (usually) store it in sidelobe file specified
via '--side', or else perform other sidelobe integrals or do other test cases,
depending on the value of <varyVersion> (for details, do '--help --calc-side').
via the option '--side'; note that sidelobe normalization factors are ignored
(see option '--side-mul').  Behavior depends on other options.  You must also
specify either '--140' ('-T') to pre-compute sidelobes for the 140-foot NRAO
telescope, or else '--GBT' ('-g') to pre-compute sidelobes for the GBT at NRAO.
  ...For <varyVersion> = 21 to 29, the behavior is as if multiple values had
been specified of 10, 11, ..., ( <varyVersion> - 10 ) for the integral below:
  ...For <varyVersion> = 10 to 20, an integral of the sidelobe weight over the
entire sphere will be calculated (for purposes of normalization; for the FULL
beam integral, you must also specify '-z 0.0 -Z 0.0'); you can specify the
beam-type via '--lin-side' (the default), '--quad-side', '--orig-side', or one
of '--sun-side' thru '--lin-2-30' thru '--spline-15' (horizon is irrelevant).
Note: integral-grid is at fractions { 1 / 2^( <varyVersion> - 10 ) } of the grid
phi = 0 (2.0) 180, theta = 0 (.01) 3 (.02) 9 (.5) 180, except: for --lin-fine-*
or --quad-fine-*: phi = 0 (0.8) 180, theta = 0 (.08) 3 (.16) 9 (.32) 180;
a value of <varyVersion> = 14, 15 or 17 gives reasonable convergence.
Note: cutoffs ARE applied (see '--zero-near' ['-z'] and '--zero-far' ['-Z']).
  ...For <varyVersion> = 110 to 120 the same thing is done [with a grid-spacing
{ 1 / 2^( <varyVersion> - 110 ) } ], but the weight from below the horizon will
also be calculated as a function of azimuth and elevation (this takes a LONG
time, probably several hours, for a reasonable value of <varyVersion> = 114;
horizon version is via '--GBT-50', '--GBT-150', '--GBT-02', or '--GBT-ground').
  ...For <varyVersion> = 30 to 37, polar (theta,phi) plots of the beam are made
with (thetaMAX,Dtheta,Dphi) = (9,0.02,2) (180,0.5,2) (9,0.02,0.5) (180,0.5,0.5)
(9,0.02,0.05) (180,0.5,0.5) (180,0.02,0.05) (180,0.02,0.5) respectively.
  ...For <varyVersion> = 41 to 55, you can specify the beam-type to make a plot
in Cartesian coordinates of that beam; half the XY-plane will show (half of)
the forward hemisphere of the beam, and the other half the backward hemisphere.
You must specify a plotfile for this output (see option '--plot-file'), but the
sidelobe file is left unchanged.  Cutoffs ARE applied (see '--zero-near').
For <varyVersion> = 50, points in the plane will be at 0.5 degree intervals.
For <varyVersion> = 51 to 55, intervals are ( <varyVersion> - 50 ) degrees.
For <varyVersion> = 41 to 49, intervals are 2^( <varyVersion> - 51 ) degrees,
and only a correspondingly-reduced central portion of the beam will be output.
  ...For <varyVersion> = 61, the beam is output in XY-coordinates (at intervals
dX = dY = 0.01-degree, out to theta = 90 degrees: values outside this are null)
as a FITS image, to the user-specified output FITS file.
  ...For <varyVersion> = 62, the same is done, except that the input 62 must
be immediately followed by values  <theta> <phi>  that indicate an alternate
position in the beam to use as the center of the plot, rather than (0.0,0.0).
  ...For <varyVersion> = 71, the same is done, but the central beam is replaced
by Sri's best calculated beam, out to R = 1.0 degrees (this requires the input
  ...For <varyVersion> of 107 or 109: SAME as 7 or 9 below, but speed up by
adding a 0.15-Gb temporary matrix, for total memory use of order 0.3 Gb.
  ...For <varyVersion> of 7: do BOTH of the cases 8 and 9 below:
  ...For <varyVersion> of 8: You must specify '--orig-side' or '--quad-fine-2.5'
['--quad-0.05-2.5'] thru '--lin-2-5' thru '--lin-2-30' thru '--quad-15' thru
'--spline-15'.  The non-isotropic part of that GBT beam at theta < 3 degrees
will be stored in the sidelobe FITS file specified by the '--side' option.
  ...For <varyVersion> of 9: similar to 8 above; but the above non-isotropic
part of the GBT beam is read in from the FITS file (or calculated if not found).
It is then convolved with an approximation to a FWHM = 9.2-arcmin gaussian (this
is VERY slow) and the convolved version is output to the FITS file.
  ...For <varyVersion> of 101, 102, 103: SAME as 1, 2, 3 below, but speed up by
adding a 0.61-Gb temporary matix, for total memory use of order 0.75 Gb
  ...OTHERWISE (for <varyVersion> of 1, 2, or 3), there are three variants:
    ...If you specify '--orig-side' (or '--quad-fine-2.5' ['--quad-0.05-2.5']
thru '--lin-2-5' thru '--lin-2-30' thru '--quad-15' thru '--spline-15'), then
THE SIDELOBE FILE IS CREATED if it does not already exist; the far-sidelobes
(convolved with a parabolic approximation to a 0.5-degree-FWHM gaussian) will
be output to this sidelobe file, on an angular grid determined by <varyVersion>:
<varyVersion> = 1 means '0.125-degree grid', 2 means '0.25-degree grid', and
3 means '0.5-degree grid'.  [This computation will take a LONG time (probably
hours or even days); the basic near-sidelobe matrix will also be output (this
is necessary for the GBT, though not for the 140-foot).]
   ...If you specify '--sun-side' or '--sun-only-side', THE SAME is done,
except that NO convolution is applied, and you can use '--out <filename>' to
specify an output sidelobe file different from the input sidelobe file.
   ...On the other hand, if you specify '--lin-side' or '--quad-side', then THE
SIDELOBE FILE MUST ALREADY EXIST; the pre-computed far sidelobe array is read
in from it, and A CONVOLVED-NEAR-SIDELOBE MATRIX IS OUTPUT TO IT (provided that
the sidelobe file was explicitly specified via '--side' ['-I']).  Here, the
input <varyVersion> has a different meaning: <varyVersion> = 1 means 'output
this convolved matrix (and if a plotfile was specified, output the full sidelobe
pattern to it)'; 2 means 'do the same (but if a plotfile was specified, output
the full sidelobe pattern to it in the near sidelobe region only)'; 3 means 'do
NOT change the sidelobe file, but if a plotfile was specified, produce a plot
of the convolved and unconvolved near sidelobe'.  Note that the convolved matrix
depends on the values of <zeroNearRadius> and <nearCutoffRadius> (see the option
'--zero-near' ['-z']); for the GBT, the value of <zeroNearRadius> must also be
equal to the value of <zeroFarRadius> (see the option '--zero-far' ['-Z']).
  --side-vers <sidelobeVersion>  (or --sidelobe-array-version-number ...): set
a specific sidelobe version number to read from the sidelobe file (for details,
do '--help --side-vers').  This option should NOT be necessary: AVOID IT.
the version-number of the sidelobe array to be looked for (the DEFAULT version
number is obtained from environment variable %s for GBT,
or %s for 140-foot; if unset, the environment variable
%s is used; if unset, a value of %d is the default).  Note
that a value of 0 has the special meaning 'read the first sidelobe array found
in the FITS file' (this is what one wants, unless the sidelobe file contains
multiple matrices), while 1 means 'require 0.125-degree angular grid', 2 means
'0.25-degree grid', and 3 means '0.5-degree grid' (other version numbers may
specify some of the sidelobe TEST CASES).  [Grid spacing affects accuracy but
not speed, and has only a minor effect on the amount of memory required.]  THIS
OPTION SHOULD NOT BE NECESSARY for any reasonable sidelobe file.
  --side-mul <sideMul>  (or --sidelobe-multiplicative-normalization-factor ...):
Renormalize the sidelobes using a multiplicative factor <sideMul> (a negative
or zero input means 'use default value': see option '--side-def-mul' below).
THIS OPTION SHOULD NEVER BE NEEDED: the normalizations have been pre-computed.
  --side-no-mul  (or --do-not-use-any-sidelobe-normalization-factor): Do not
renormalize the sidelobes (equivalent to '--side-mul 1.0').  AVOID THIS OPTION.
  --side-def-mul  (or --use-default-sidelobe-normalization-factor): Renormalize
the sidelobes using a default value for the multiplicative normalization factor.
This compensates for the fact that the formulae/data-files giving the telescope
beam may have an integral (over 4 * PI) that is not exactly unity.
(unstored) cases, the integral is computed to obtain the normalization.  (For
details, do '--help --side-def-mul'.)  This option is the DEFAULT case.
This option is the DEFAULT case, so this option can be omitted.
For the GBT with '--lin-side' (the default case), this normalization factor is
<sideMul> = %.7f (for <sidelobeVersion> = 901, as read from sidelobe file),
    %.7f for 701, %.7f for 301, and %.7f for 201; the GBT with
'--quad-side' => %.7f, %.7f, %.7f, and %.7f, respectively.
Other GBT cases: '--orig-side' => %.7f, '--sun-side' => %.7f,
 '--sun-only-side' => %.7f, '--lin-fine-2.5' => %.7f,
 '--quad-fine-2.5' => %.7f, '--lin-2-5' => %.7f,
 '--quad-2-5' => %.7f, '--lin-2-30' => %.7f,
 '--quad-2-30' => %.7f, '--lin-15' => %.7f,
 '--quad-15' => %.7f, '--spline-15' => %.7f,
For the 140-foot telescope with '--lin-side' (the default case), this factor is
<sideMul> = %.7f (for <sidelobeVersion> = %d with <nearCutoffRadius> = 5.0).
Other 140-foot cases: alternate case '--lin-side' => %.7f for (%d, 9.0),
 '--quad-side' => %.7f for (%d, 5.0), or %.7f for (%d, 9.0), and
 '--orig-side' => %.7f for <nearCutoffRadius> = 5.0, or %.7f for 9.0.
NOTE that for '--lin-side' or '--quad-side', the normalization depends of the
value of <sidelobeVersion> that has been read in from the sidelobe file (see
options '--side-vers' and '--calc-side'); for <sidelobeVersion> values other
than those given above, a reasonable integral of the beam over all solid angle
will be performed (taking a minute or two) to obtain the normalization factor
(for this case, if you have a more accurate value, '--side-mul' may be useful).
For the 140-foot telescope, such a beam integration will also be performed
if <nearCutoffRadius> is neither 5.0 nor 9.0 (see option '--zero-near').
  --set-side-int <Sint>  (or --set-sidelobe-part-of-beam-integral ...): set the
fraction <Sint> of an unknown telescope beam that is considered to yield stray
radiation (must not exceed 0.3), rather than calculating it from the beam.
should ONLY be used to input a pre-computed non-default sidelobe integral: this
input value of <Sint> will be IGNORED if some stored default value is available.
This input value WILL be multiplied as usual by any <sideFac> beam normalization
factor (whether the default one or input via the '--side-mul' option), UNLESS
you specify both '--correct-stray' and one of the '--cmp' options.
AVOID USING THIS OPTION: it is not needed when you use a known telescope beam,
and will be calculated automatically for an unknown telescope beam.
  --set-wtot <Wtot>  (or --set-total-sidelobe-weight-fraction ...): set the
fraction WTOTSDLB of the telescope beam that is considered to yield stray
This input value will NOT be multiplied by any <sideFac> beam normalization
factor (whether the default one or input via the '--side-mul' option), i.e., it
is useful when the main beam power is known better than the sidelobes.
  --zero-near <zeroNearRadius> <nearCutoffRadius>  (or -z ...
or --zero-near-sidelobe-radius ...): reset the radius <zeroNearRadius> (in
degrees) containing the region considered to be part of the main beam, not the
sidelobes (for details, do '--help --zero-near').
  --zero-far <zeroFarRadius>  (or -Z ... or --zero-far-sidelobe-radius ...):
equal to <zeroNearRadius> by default (for details, do '--help --zero-far').
degrees) out to which the near-sidelobe beam array will be set to zero: inside
<zeroNearRadius> is considered to be part of the main beam, not the sidelobes (a
value of more than 4.0 is an error; a value of -9.0 or less will be reset to
the DEFAULT of %.3f for the GBT or %.3f for 140-foot).  If <nearCutoffRadius>
is omitted, the DEFAULT of %.3f for the GBT or %.3f for 140-foot is used;
otherwise, this value resets the outer cutoff radius (in degrees) beyond which
the near-sidelobe beam array is not used (i.e., is set to zero); a value of 9.0
means 'use the entire available near-sidelobe matrix'.  Note that, in internal
storage, the beam is divided (for convenience) into a 'far' part (which is in
general fairly smooth and relatively weak) and a 'near' part that includes the
main beam (and thus is much stronger and far from smooth, and is thus stored in
a more finely-spaced 2D array).  [It is only for test purposes that one can set
<zeroFarRadius> different from <zeroNearRadius>, via '--zero-far' below.]
reset the radius (in degrees) out to which the 'far' part of the sidelobe beam
will be set to zero (a value of more than 4.0 is an error; a value of -9.0 or
less will be set to the DEFAULT, i.e., equal to <zeroNearRadius>).
  --side-add-gauss <Fgauss> <FWHMgauss>  (or -G ...
or --add-gaussian-lobe-to-sidelobe ...): TEST CASE: multiply original sidelobe
(telescope beam) by a factor of ( 1.0 - <Fgauss> ), and add a gaussian lobe
with a total weight of <Fgauss> (negative input value yields %.6f) and a
FWHM of <FWHMgauss> degrees (negative or missing input yields default of %.3f).
The <zeroFarRadius> cutoff is used at small theta (see '--zero-far').
  --side-add-iso <Fiso>  (or -i ... or --add-isotropic-lobe-to-sidelobe ...):
TEST CASE: multiply the original sidelobe by a factor of ( 1.0 - <Fiso> ), and
add an isotropic lobe with full weight <Fiso> (negative input yields %.6f).
[With both: the original sidelobe is multiplied by ( 1.0 - <Fgauss> - <Fiso> ).]
  --side-no-fudge  (or --no-spillover-fudge-factor): do not use any fudge
factor that distinguishes between the forward spillover lobe and the rest of
the GBT beam (this is the DEFAULT case, and need not be specified).
  --side-fudge <Fin> <Fspill> <Fout> <FspUP>  (or --spillover-fudge-factors ...
or --inner-spillover-outer-fudge-factors ...): TEST CASE: specify fudge factors
for the GBT forward spillover lobe
 (for details, do '--help --side-fudge').
  --fudge-ellipse-in <Ain> <Bin> <Cin> <Din>  (or --inner-fudge-ellipse ...
or --inner-fudge-ellipse-parameters ...):
 reset the definition of the inner edge
of the GBT forward spillover lobe (for details, do '--help --fudge-ellipse-in').
  --fudge-ellipse-out <Aout> <Bout> <Cout> <Dout>  (or --outer-fudge-ellipse ...
or --outer-fudge-ellipse-parameters ...):
 reset the definition of the outer edge
of the GBT forward spillover lobe (do '--help --fudge-ellipse-out' for details).
: <Fin> for the inner (non-spillover-lobe)
region, <Fspill> for the approximate spillover-lobe region, and <Fout> for the
(non-spillover-lobe) region further out.  If <FspUP> is given and is not unity,
then a gradient will be applied to <Fspill> as a function of the angle around
the center of its inner boundary, yielding a total factor of <Fspill> * <FspUP>
in the upward direction and <Fspill> / <FspUP> in the downward one.  Negative or
missing input values yield the DEFAULT values (namely, <Fin> = %.3f,
<Fspill> = %.3f, <Fout> = <Fin>, and <FspUP> = 1.0).
 set the position and width of the
ellipse defining the inner edge of the GBT forward spillover lobe in degrees;
negative or missing inputs yield DEFAULT values, namely <Ain> = %.3f for
the semi-(major) axis along the vertical direction, <Bin> = %.3f for the
semi-(minor) axis (roughly horizontal), <Cin> = %.3f for the vertical offset
from the beam center, and <Din> = %.3f for the width of the fudge-factor
switchover region (measured outwards from the given ellipse), in which the
fudge factor will be intermediate between <Fin> and <Fspill>.
ellipse delimiting the outer edge of the GBT forward spillover lobe, similarly
(negative or missing inputs yield DEFAULT values, namely, <Aout> = %.3f,
<Bout> = %.3f, <Cout> = %.3f, and <Dout> = %.3f).  Note that it is an
error if any part of this outer ellipse overlaps with the inner-ellipse's
switchover region (of width <Din> outwards from the specified inner ellipse).
Note also that for '--lin-side' or '--quad-side' the given values of <Ain>,
<Bin>, <Aout>, and <Bout> will all be reduced by 0.5 degrees, while <Din> and
<Dout> will be increased by 1.0 degrees (to account approximately for the
effect that the 0.5-degree-FWHM convolution would have had).
  --lin-side  (or --linear-sidelobe-interpolation): linear interpolation in
sidelobe values from near and far matrices is used [these are read in from a
pre-computed sidelobe file, whose name is specified by environment variables or
via the option '--side'].  This is the DEFAULT case: this option can be omitted.
  --quad-side  (or --biquadratic-sidelobe-interpolation): similar, except that
bi-quadratic interpolation is used (much like a spline, but faster).
  --orig-side  (or --compute-original-sidelobe): use the original computed
version of the sidelobe rather than pre-computed values (this is slower; also,
unlike the above versions, this is NOT convolved with a 0.5-degree FWHM gaussian
that would smooth the sidelobe on a scale similar to the LD-survey beam and thus
avoid possible spurious effects from small-scale "wiggles" in the sidelobe).
  --sun-side <FnormSun>  (or --sun-scan-sidelobe-extended-by-calculated-beam
...): use the GBT sun-scan files to give the sidelobe in the region where these
sun-scans exist (the curent directory must contain the 3 default sun-scan files
as well as all of the 'sunscan_####_SPA.txt' files); if <FnormSun> is specified
(and non-negative), it is used to convert from sun-scans in K to beam values
(replacing the default multiplier of %.3f).  Beyond this sun-scan region
(i.e., far from the beam center, where the sidelobe is very small), extend
this empirical beam using the beam values from the calculated GBT beam pattern.
NOTE THAT, while the factor <FnormSun> is not applied to this calculated-beam
extension, any normalization factor relevant to the calculated beam is applied
to the entire beam, INCLUDING the empirical beam region.
  --sun-only-side <FnormSun>  (or --sun-scan-sidelobe-with-no-extension ...):
use the GBT sun-scans to give the sidelobe (as for '--sun-side' above) in the
sun-scan region; but set the sidelobe to zero outside this region.
  --lin-fine-2.5  (or --linear-sidelobe-0.05-deg-cuts-subreflector-2.5-deg):
[TEST CASE]: For the GBT, use sidelobes interpolated linearly from 0.05-degree
cuts in phi [with 0.02-degree spacing in theta, 0.01-deg for theta < 3.0 deg]
('fine': resolving all structure in the computed beam), where the input
subreflector scattered patterns [originally used to calculate the GBT beam]
had used 2.5-degree cuts in phi.  (Again, this beam is NOT convolved with a
  --quad-fine-2.5  (or --biquad-sidelobe-0.05-deg-cuts-subreflector-2.5-deg):
[TEST CASE]: The same as '--lin-fine-2.5', except that the sidelobes are
interpolated bi-quadratically rather than linearly.
  --lin-2-5  (or --linear-sidelobe-2-deg-cuts-subreflector-5-deg): [TEST CASE]:
For the GBT, use sidelobes interpolated linearly from 2-degree cuts in phi (with
0.5-degree theta-spacing at theta > 9 degrees), where the input subreflector
scattered patterns had used 5-degree cuts in phi.
  --quad-2-5  (or --biquad-sidelobe-2-deg-cuts-subreflector-5-deg): [TEST CASE]:
The same as '--lin-2-5', except sidelobes are interpolated bi-quadratically.
  --lin-2-30  (or --linear-sidelobe-2-deg-cuts-subreflector-30-deg): [TEST
CASE]: The same as '--lin-2-5', but with a less-accurate input beam calculation
(where the input subreflector scattered patterns had used 30-degree phi-cuts).
  --quad-2-30  (or --biquad-sidelobe-2-deg-cuts-subreflector-30-deg): [TEST
CASE]: The same as '--lin-2-30', except that the sidelobes are interpolated
bi-quadratically from the 2-degree cuts.
  --lin-15  (or --linear-sidelobe-from-15-degree-cuts): [TEST CASE]: For the
GBT, use the oldest available sidelobes, interpolated linearly from 15-degree
cuts in phi (with 0.5-degree theta intervals along each cut).
  --quad-15  (or --biquadratic-sidelobe-from-15-degree-cuts): [TEST CASE]: For
the GBT, use sidelobes interpolated bi-quadratically from 15-degree cuts.
  --spline-15  (or --spline-sidelobe-from-15-degree-cuts): [TEST CASE]: For
the GBT, use sidelobes interpolated by cubic spline from 15-degree cuts.
  --isotropic  (or --isotropic-sidelobe): [TEST CASE]: Instead of using the
relevant telescope's sidelobe weight, just use an isotropic value equal to the
solid angle divided by 4*PI, for all points above the horizon.  Note that this
value will be multiplied by any sidelobe normalization factor (supplied via the
option '--side-mul', and will be reduced smoothly to zero as one gets closer to
the beam-direction than <zeroFarRadius> (see option '--zero-far').
  --with-x-pol  (or --include-cross-pol-contribution-to-beam): include both
the principal polarization and the cross polarization contributions to the GBT
telescope beam and sidelobes, for cases '--lin-fine-2.5' thru '--quad-2-30'
(this is the DEFAULT case: it need not be specified).
  --no-x-pol  (or --do-not-include-cross-pol-contribution-to-beam): instead,
include only the principal polarization contribution for these cases.
  --npix-140 <Npix>  (or --set-140-foot-near-sidelobe-matrix-pixel-flag ...):
For the original (unconvolved) 140-foot sidelobe calculation (see '--orig-side'
and '--140'),this flag determines how the near-sidelobe matrix will be handled
(for details, do '--help --npix-140').  Useful mainly for TEST CASES.
For the old (unconvolved) 140-foot sidelobe calculation, this flag determines
how the near sidelobe matrix will be handled.  [NOTE: this option has no effect
unless you also specify '--orig-side' as well as '--140' ('-T')].  <Npix> = -1
averages a square area in the matrix of size +/- 9 pixels (19 pixels square,
i.e., 0.4974 degrees square) about the 'best match' [the pixel nearest to the
(theta,phi) position in the beam where the sidelobe value is to be returned];
this is the DEFAULT, and yields the original 140-foot result as per Ed Murphy's
original version of this program.  <Npix> = 0 or higher does the same, but over
an area of +/- <Npix> pixels, (2 * <Npix> + 1) pixels square (note: <Npix> = 0
just gets the 'best match' pixel).  <Npix> = -2 interpolates the near sidelobe
matrix biquadratically to get the value at the (theta,phi) position in the beam
(similar to <Npix> = 0, but more accurate).  <Npix> = -3 is similar, except for
using linear interpolation (if you have specified '--calc-side 10' or higher
[to get plots or integrals of the beam], then THIS is the DEFAULT case).
<Npix> = -9 zeros the near sidelobe matrix (only the far sidelobe contributes).
YOU SHOULD NOT NEED THIS OPTION, unless you are computing a modified sidelobe
matrix for the 140-foot telescope, or doing some sort of test case.
  --all  (or --access-all-scans-in-input-file): process ALL the scans in the
input scan-file [or that subset selected by the range options '--index-range',
'--not-object', and/or '--object-range'].
  (For details, do '--help --all').
  --batch <batchFilename>  (or -b ... or --batch-scan-number-input-filename ..):
For SDD-files: specify a text batch-file from which to read the scan-numbers to
be processed (do '--help --batch' for details).
  If you are computing strayscans
(sidelobe corrections), then those spectra that do not appear already to be
strayscans or stray-subtracted scans will have strayscans calculated (note: for
SDD-files, a scan-number that has a fractional part of .39 or less indicates
that it should be precessed, except that, if a scan-number incremented by 0.01
is found, this latter scan is not processed separately).  This option is the
DEFAULT case (provided that you do not specify '--interactive').
provide the name of a text batch-file holding scan-numbers to be processed, one
per line, until a negative value or end-of-file is encountered (this is useful
only for SDD files from the 140-foot telescope, where the scan number is a
unique identifier).  Note that specifying '--batch -' (or '-b -') is equivalent
to '--batch-stdin' ('-B'), and specifying '--batch +' (or '-b +') is equivalent
to '--all'.  [In no case will strayscans (sidelobe corrections) be calculated
for scan-numbers with fractional parts of .40 or more (but see '--force').]
  --batch-stdin  (or -B or --batch-scan-numbers-from-standard-input):
similar to '--batch' ('-b'), but SDD-file scan-numbers to be processed will be
prompted for and read from the standard input instead of from a batch-file (this
option acts independently of the option '--interactive').
  --interactive  : prompt for the scan-input, scan-output, and batch filenames
(unless they were specified on the command line), and prompt to correct errors.
  --good-precess  (or --use-Julian-year-for-precession): use the correct formula
when calculating precession.  This is the DEFAULT case (need not be specified).
  --orig-precess  (or --use-approximate-year-length-for-precession): use an
approximate year-length value when calculating precession, as a TEST CASE (this
should not have much effect on the results).
  --good-lat  (or --corrected-140-foot-latitude): for the 140-foot telescope at
NRAO, use the updated geodetic and geocentric latitude values.  This is the
DEFAULT case, so this option can be omitted.
  --orig-lat  (or --original-140-foot-latitude): for the 140-foot telescope at
NRAO, use the original (less-accurate) latitude value as both geodetic and
geocentric latitude (this option does not affect the GBT).  Note that doing
this TEST CASE has negligible effect.
  --good-tau  (or --tau-corrected-formula): use the corrected formula for
atmospheric attenuation.  This is the DEFAULT case (option can be omitted).
  --orig-tau  (or --tau-original-formula): TEST CASE:use the original formula
for the atmospheric optical depth tau, which greatly overestimates atmospheric
attenuation at low elevation angles; NOT RECOMMENDED.
  --n-tau <Ntau>  (or --tau-array-size ...): set the size of the array used to
interpolate the above formulae (negative value yields the DEFAULT of %d; the
minimum allowed positive value is %d; if you set <Ntau> = 0, then the actual
formulae will always be used, rather than interpolation in a stored array).
  --sub-lim <negKlim>  (or --input-spectrum-validity-limit ...): set the
'bad-input-spectrum-value' upper limit when subtracting strayscans [SHOULD NOT
BE NEEDED, since bad (null) data should be "UNDEFINED" (NaN) in the input].
'bad-input-spectrum-value' when subtracting strayscans (the DEFAULT is -1.0E+6);
if '--correct-stray' was specified and any input spectrum value is less than
or equal to <negKlim>, then it is assumed that this is a 'bad-spectrum-value'
flag, and it is copied unchanged to the output stray-subtracted file [normally,
THIS OPTION SHOULD NOT BE NEEDED, since bad spectrum values would be flagged by
"UNDEFINED" (NaN) values in the input file].  A positive or zero input
value resets <negKlim> to the default of %.1e (note that any strayscan
output will be computed irrespective of whether the input spectrum is 'bad').
  --little-endian : ensure that any (new) binary output SDD-file will have
byte-ordering appropriate for a little-endian computer, byte-swapping on output
if necessary.  If the output SDD-file already exists and is appended to, this
option is ignored.  Note that endian options have no effect on SDFITS-files.
  --big-endian : ensure big-endian output SDD-file byte-ordering, similarly.
  --keep-endian : use the same byte-ordering for a new output SDD-file as in
the input SDD-file.  This is the present default.
  --local-endian : any new (or overwritten) output SDD-file will have the
byte-ordering appropriate for the computer on which this program is running.
  --fix-date  (or --fix-bad-10-second-value-in-DATE-OBS): interpret a value of
':0****' seconds at the end of the DATE-OBS string value as ':10.00' seconds
(fix for a bug in the gbtidl software, which may write ':0****' for some values
from 9.99 to 10.00 seconds).  This is the DEFAULT case (need not be specified).
  --no-fix-date  (or --crash-when-bad-10-second-value-in-DATE-OBS): do not use
the above fix (present for compatibility with old program versions; the sidelobe
program will crash if it finds a DATE-OBS with ':0****').  NOT RECOMMENDED.
  --fix-date-warn <NwarnDATE>  (or --fix-date-N-warn ...
or --Nwarn-of-fix-bad-10-second-value-in-DATE-OBS ...): the same as the default
case ('--fix-date'), except that a warning message will be output for scans
where a DATE-OBS with ':0****' is found (upper limit of <NwarnDATE> messages).
  --use-amb-scan  (or --scanfile-ambient-conditions-override-defaults): if the
input scan-file contains values for ambient temperature, pressure, relative
humidity, or dew point, these will override the default values (see the option
'--amb-set-def').  This is the DEFAULT case (need not be specified).
  --force-amb-def  (or --default-ambient-conditions-override-scanfile): TEST
CASE: any ambient conditions contained in the input scan-file will be ignored
completely (though the defaults will still be overridden by any obtained from
an ambient-conditions file, if one was specified via the option '--amb').
  --get-sun-pos <lon> <lat> <el>
(or --get-sun-position-for-lonTel-latTel-deg-elTel-m ...): For a telescope at
the given longitude/latitude (in degrees: negative is south/west) and elevation
(in meters), interactively ask for date and time (UT) values; for each input
date/time, output the position of the Sun as computed via various algorithms.
If the the telescope position <lon> <lat> <el> is not given, the default is to
use the position of the GBT telescope.
  --gbt-sun-scan <DindexSun> <addSLALIBpos>
(or --process-GBT-telescope-sun-scan-ASCII-file ...): indicates that the file
specified by '--in' is an ASCII sun-scan file from the GBT, containing the
Modified Julian Date (in sec), the RA and DEC of the beam (in degrees), and
antenna temperatures Ta:Rx0 and Ta:Rx1 (in K), while the file specified by
'--out' is a modified version of this with the scan number (plus <DindexSun> if
this is specified), the GBT azimuth and elevation, and the position of the Sun
(obtained via the SPA algorithm).  If <addSLALIBpos> is specified and positive,
the Sun's position via SLALIB is also output (this latter appears to be buggy).
  --gbt-sun-cross <NsunType> <SunscanFile_1> ... <SunscanFile_NsunType>
(or --get-crossings-for-processed-GBT-sun-scan-file ...): indicates that the
file specified by '--in' contains one or more processed sun-scans (as produced
via '--gbt-sun-scan' above).  For each such sun-scan, the scans that cross it
will be found in the files <SunscanFile_1> thru <SunscanFile_NsunType>, and the
crossing points will be output to the file specified by '--out'.  The input
value <NsunType> must not exceed %d; if it is negative, then its absolute value
is used, and the <NsunType> filenames will not be read in (the default names
will be used, for old vertical, new vertical, and new horizontal sun-scans,
respectively).  Note that for each sun-scan (of index ####) in these files, the
file "sunscan_####_SPA.txt" must exist, containing the cleaned sun-scan.
   If you had PREVIOUSLY specified '--gbt-sun-scan <DindexSun>', then the last
<DindexSun> of these Sunscan Files will be ignored (used only for bridging gaps
in the input file); if <DindexSun> is negative, then  [ abs(<DindexSun>) - 1 ]
will be used, and only the interpolated crossing points will be output (rather
than a range of lines about each crossing).  NOTE that if <DindexSun> is NOT
less than <NsunType>, then no crossings will be computed, and a smoothed
version of the input file will be produced instead (in this latter case, the
input scans must be contained in these Sunscan Files).
